894.33
Referring to my memoranaum of December 86, 1929,
reporting the results of my conversations With Captain
Furlong, sureau of Naval Operations, and Major Roland F.
Walsh, Quartermaster eneral's Office, in connection
With the question of visits of American transports to
Japanese ports, Mr. Miller and I called today on
Colonel Knox, uartermaster General's Office, to make
3
further inquiry in regard to the arrangements with the
Japanese Government relating to the calls of army
transports at Japanese ports. Colonel Knox said that
he had peen unable to locate the War Department's
files on this subject but stated it was his under-
standing that the calls of the army transports at
Nagasaki and other Japanese ports werea part of a
special arrangement with the Japanese Government under
which a Quartermaster's Office was established in 1898
at
FB251930
FILED

=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
at Nagasaki. During the period of the World War
this Office was temporarily suspended but revived
after the war under a new arrangement with the
Japanese Government in 1921.
The information sup-
plied oy Colonel Inox enabled me, subsequently, to
locate the Department's own correspondence in regard
to this subject, which was found to confirm the state-
ments made by Colonel Knox.
He expressed the opinion that there was ne good
reason why it vas necessary to insist upon notifica-
tion being made throuh diplomatic channels in connec-
tion with visits of unarmed puulic vessels and he
hoped it would be possiule to establish an arrangement
on a mutually satisfactory basis for the elimination
of all unnecessary formalities in connection with
such visits.
In view of the attitude thus expressed by Colonel
Knox ana also by Captain Furlong, of the Bureau of
Naval Operations, there would seem to be no reason why
the Department should not accede to the request of the
Japanese Enbassy that the formality of notification
through diplomatic channels be dispensed with in connec-
tion with the visits of unarmed
vessels, such as
transports

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.94)
- 3 -
transports,to Philippine ports as well as to other
ports under American jurisdiction not closed to
foreign vessels, on the basis of reciprocity. If
you concur in this view, it is believed that it would
be desirable that the matter be taken up informally with
the Bureau of Insular Affairs of the War Department be-
fore formal action is taken in the matter. If the
Bureau of Insular Affairs approves, a formal letter may
be sent to the War Department, as well as to the Navy
Department, setting forth the action contemplated in
view of the Japanese Embassy's request. Upon the re-
ceipt of their replies, the decision of the Government
can be communicated to the Japanese Embassy.
223
Rauu,

The matter dealt with in these letters
has been the subject of several written
communications between the Departments con-
cerned and  number of conferences to
which officers of the Navy Department, of-
ficers of the War Department and officers
of SO and FE in this Department have been
parties. These two letters represent what,
with the replies which come, will probably
dispose of the matter so far as inter-depart-
mental consideration is concerned and wiil
enable this Department to tare the final
step which would consist in a conference be-
tween an officer of this Division and an
officer of the Japanese Embassy, wherein our
conclusion would be communicated orally to
the Japanese.

With reference to this Department's letter of

3
311
by Japanese public vessels in visiting ports in the
Philippine Islands, I understand that as a result of
143
several conferences since held between various offieers
of the Quartermaster's Department of the Army, of the
a
Office of Naval Operations of your Department and of
opinion that we might reasonably aocede to a recent re-
quest made orally by the Japanese Embassy in Washington
894.3311
that we revert to the former praotice of permitting
Japanese transports and other unarmed public vessels to
visit ports in the Philippine Islands upon notification
of such visite being given through the Japanese Consul
General at Manila to the Governor-General rather than
threugh diplomatie ohannels to the Department of State,

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
and without reference by the Governor-General to
Washington. The procedure with respect to armed publio
vessels should, it is believed, remain as at present.
One reason for the conelusion reached in relation
to unarmed public vessels is that the proposed arrange-
ment would simplify the procedure in such cases and would
appear to be as much to the advantage of our transport
service, calling at Japanese ports, as to the Japanese
when calling at Philippine ports. An additional reason
for taking the proposed action at this time is the fact
that, as recently reported by our Embassy in Tokyo, the
Japanese authorities appear now to be developing a more
conciliatory attitude on the question of the visits of
American naval vessels to the Japenese mandated islands
in the Pacific.
If you and the Secretary of War coneur in this view
and see no objection to reverting to the former practice.
this Department will bring the matter to a conclusion
informally with the Japanese Embassy.
Very truly yours,
J. P. Cotton
Acting Secretary ef State.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
143B
f
With reference to letter from the Department of State,
3
dated January 29, 1930, in regard to the procedure to be
followed by Japanese public vessels in visit ing ports in
3
the Philippine Islands, wherein it is stated that the Jap-
anese Embassy in Washington has recently requested that a
reversion to the former practice of permitting Japanese
transports and other unarmed public vessels to visit ports
in the Philip ine Islands upon notification of such visits
f
being given through the Japanese Consul General at Manila to
the Governor General rather than through diplomatic channels
to the Department of State, and without reference by the Governor
General to Washington. The letter also expresses the view of the
State Department that the procedure with respect to vis its of
armed public vessels should remain as at present, and invites ah
FEB
expression of the views of tie Var Department relative to the pro-
posed procedure.
2
9
The matter was referred to the Governor General of the Philip-
19
36
pine Islands by cablegram, ana this Department is now in receipt
of a cablegram (No. 86, February 11) in which the Governor General
states that he has no objection to the proposal outlined above. The
Governor General also includes in his reply the statement of certain
views of the Command ing General, Philippine Department, relative to
the matter of visits of armed public vessels.
The War Department concurs in the views set forth in the above
letter of January 29th, from the Department of State, and sees no
objection to reverting to the former practice in regard to unarmed
Japanese public vessels. This Department is of the opinion that the
procedure with respect to visits of armed public vessels should re-
main as at present.
Sincerely yours
Tatie Hurley
Secretary of War.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
Reference is made to State Department letter of 17 December,
C
1927, and to State Department letter of 24 February, 1930 (file FE
C
894.3311/147). The first of these letters stated that "the Secretary
of War is also being informed that, should the authorities at Manila
in future receive from the Japanese Consul General any further requests
for visits similar to that of the RYOKAI MARU, it would be appreciated
if this Department were to be informed and the Japanese Consul General
5
notified that the matter should be taken up through diplomatic channels."
2
The second reference stated that our government had assented to a proposal
of the Japanese government to revert to a former procedure whereby public
vessels of Japan other than men-of-war might visit Philippine ports by
notification to the Governor-General through the Japanese Consul General
at Manila, and United States public vessels other than men-of-war might
visit Japanese ports by notification through the American Embassy at Tokio.
In response to an inquiry from this Department, the Commandant,
Sixteenth Naval District, has reported that during 1929 Japanese public
vessels made the following visits to Philippine ports,all by notification
to the Governor-General or other local authority, and without notification
through diplomatic channels:

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
It would appear that the Commandant's information with respect
to the manner of notification for the visits during 1929 and 1930 is not
entirely correct. As stated in your letter of 26 September. l929, at least
one of the visits listed for that year - that of the SHUNKOTSU MARU - was
by diplomatic notification: and it does not appear that the Navy Department
has received from the State Department any information of diplomatic noti-
fication regarding the visits listed for 1930. In this matter, however, the
State Department will have authoritative information from its own records.
8112394129-130.131.135-136
In connection with plans for the U.S.S. GOLD STAR, station ship
at Guam, to visit Miiki from 7 to 10 December and Kobe from 12 to 16 December,
the Commander-in-Chief, Asiatic Fleet, advises that the Japanese Foreign Office
has informed the American Embassy unofficially that permission has not yet been
granted for such vessels to visit Japanese ports without formal notification
through diplomatic channels, although the agreement to this new procedure would
be given shortly by the Japanese government.
If no objection is perceived, it is requested that the Japanese
government be notified of the proposed informal visits of the GOLD STAR as
stated above, and that as a separate issue the understanding between the two
governments on this subject be clarified.
In connection with the general subject of the understanding between
the United States government and the Japanese government with respect to visits
of public vessels, and in particular the visits of our vessels to the Japanese
mandated islands, it is of interest to note that Japanese naval tankers have
been habitually visiting our west coast ports and Hawaii without diplomatic
notifioation. According to the records of this Department approximately forty
such visits have been made since l926, notification in each case having been
given informally from the Japanese naval attache at Washington to the Navy De-
partment. So far as this Department is aware, no general permission for this
procedure has been granted. The Navy Department sees no objection to a contin-
uation of this procedure, but suggests that it may be used as a basis for a
reciprocal arrangement in connection with the visits of our naval vessels to
the Japanese mandated islands, which subject is of immediate interest in connec-
tion with a proposed visit of the U.S.S. HOUSTON as notified to the State De-
partment by Navy Department letter of 3 November, 1930, (file CL30/A4-3(1)(30l0l3).
811.3394/137
Sincerely your,
CAlas
Honorable H. L. Stimson,
Seoretary of State,
Washington, D.C.

of State
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
SUBJECT:
Japanese naval oil tanker en route to Hawaiian waters.
F/H
TO:
The Adjutant General, Washington, D. C.
S
94.3311
1. Local Japanese newspapers report the projected arrival at
the port of Honolulu on Febru ry 12th of the Japanese naval oil tank-
894.20211
er "Erimo," en route from Japan to the Pacific Coast for a cargo of
894.3311
fuel oil. This vessel is an auxiliary of the regular Japanese Navy,
and is manned by regul r nuval personnel - a normel complement of a
tanker bein: about 20 officers and 100 sailors.
2. While this headquarters has no definite information to in-
dicate that the projected call of the "Erimo" at this time is any-
thing but a coincidence, the present disturbed conditions in the
Orient prompts sending the information for what it is worth. More-
over, the following from official records here is thought to be of
particular interest at this time.
5
8
3. Japanese naval oil tankers began calling at Honolulu and
other island ports in the year 1917 and, up to the Spring of 1923,
several tankers made intermittent calls here en route to and from
the coast. The vanguard of what for several years became known
as the regular schedule of a fleet of these tankers, operating
between Japan and the Pacific Coast, arrived at Honolulu in April,
1923. During that year a total of 9 tankers called. Subsequent
years showed the following number of calls: 1924,10; 1925,11;
Confidentia
1926,14; 1927,7; 1928,8; 1929,7; 1930,3; 1931,2. The 1ast visit
of a tanker here was in February, 1931.
4. In a further search of the records it is revealed that,
during Joint Army-Navy Exercise No. 3, in the Spring of 1925, the
ing shortly thereafter, exactly three hours prior to the conclusion
of the maneuvers - during the period of the so-called mass landing
2
expedition of the Blue Forces on the north coast of Oahu. The
"Hayatono" was said to have hovered on the flank of the "Blue Fleet
all the way across the Pacific from San Francisco to Honolulu. In
May, 1928, when the American battle fleet visited the islands three
other Japanese tankers made unusual calls at Honolulu.+ It being

=== Page 37 === (confidence 0.99)
during the late spring season it was understood from announced schedules
that during this period of ordinarily good weather, the tankers would
omit Honolulu as a port of call and travel direct to the Pacific Coast
over the Great Circle route.
5. Several years' observation warrants the definite conclusion
that the frequent changing of the commissioned personnel on the tankers
calling here is for the purpose of permitting a greater number of of-
ficers to note the geographic, port and harbor facilities. and other
894.20211
features of Oahu. Furthermore, these tankers, during the past few years,
have been observed, habitually, in Hawaiian waters during the holding
of maneuvers, and when international Pan-Pacific gatherings are being
held. In addition, the arrival of Japanese n val vessels al ays is the
incentive for admiration and n tionalistic display on the part of local
Japanese.
6. On June 5, 1923, a battery of the 55th Coast Artillery was
conducting calibrution firing from the shore at Fort Kamehameha, at
a range of approximately 10,000 yards. During the firing the tanker
"Iro" arrived off shore from the Orient. She narrowly escaped being
hit with shells because she steamed direct for the harbor entrance,
either through ignorance of the firing or through mistaken signals.
The incident caused some discussion in the Japanese press, but it
was adjusted with the Japanese Consulate General, and newspaper ac-
counts of the incidents were then minimized.
7. Japanese naval oil tankers passing through here huve fre-
personnel, civilians who are described as "special visitors,""in-
structors," etc. In reality, however, there is every reason to be-
lieve that they are Japanese intelligence agents. Being regul r na-
894.20211
val vessels or a foreign power the tankers are usually accorded the
894.20211a
courtesy of the port by Customs and Immigration authorities. This
arrangement makes it easy for persons to leave naval vessels here
and remain for an indefinite period, or, as is generally thought to
be the case, lay over for a period and catch a following naval ves-
sel.
For the Commanding General:
J. H. HILLS,
Major,A.G.D.
Asst. Adjutant General

I have the honor to renort that folloving an exchange
of official calls at Victoria on Arril 4 the Jaranese Training
Squadron, consisting of two shins, the YAYuMo and the THATy,
68
under the command of Vice Admiral Cengo Pakutake, arri ed at
Vancouver on the morning of April 5 and that their deporture
for San Francisco at 3 PM on the 7th was reported by the
Consulate General to the Chief of Staff of the Thirteenth Naval
MAY 1-1933
District, Seattle, Washington, in a telegrarhic renly to a letter
of April 3 in which he reguented that information as to the
exact time of the den rture of these ships from Vancouver be so
telegraphed to him, in order that he in turn might inform the
Navy Department as to the probable time of the arrivel of the
ships in San Trancisco.
At the time of the Squadron's arrival Vice Admiral Ayakutake
issued the following st temcnt:
We are here on a friendly vicit to cxpresc
to you our earnest desire to co-opcrate with you
in fulfilling our common mission in the Pacific,
The

=== Page 58 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
"The principal duty of our Training Squadron,
as its name shows, is to give training to the young
midshipmen for their future career, yet I wish to
give our crews the opportunities of elevating their
experiences and knowledge by getting acquainted with
Canada and her people, in whom they are so much in-
terested.
"The ocean was not placed there for us to
quarrel over. It is there for our benefit and ad-
vancement. We are happy to see, so far as the Dominion
of Canada and Japan are concerned, that there has been
a steady growth of appreciation and understanding.
"Towards the Dominion of Canada, we have always
had a natural feeling of friendship and admiration, so
that our present visit is altogether congenial.
"It is my earnest wish, as it is my greatest hope,
to promote better and friendly relations between this
country and that which I have the honor to serve."
On the evening of the 5th Vice Admiral Hyalutake and the
officers of his staff and of the VAKUMO and the IWATE were
tendered a banquet by the Mayor and City Council of Vancouver
at which the other guests, with the sole exception of the
American Consul General, were representative Canadian and
Japanese residents of Vancouver, Canadian officials, naval
and military predominating, and the Japanese Naval Attaches at
Ottawa and Washington.
Japanese and Canadian speakers at the banquet in their
exchange of expressions of goodwill in the name of their respec-
tive countries emphasized the importance of the maintenance
of cordial relations between Canada and Japan as Pacific neigh-
bors.
On April 5, following the banquet, the Acting Japanese
Consul held a reception in the ballroom of the Hotel Vancouver
at which there was a large attendance of Dominion and Provincial
officials, consular officers and representative citizens of
British

=== Page 59 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
British Columbia.
On the afternoon of April 6 Vice Admiral Hyakutake and
his staff received a considerable number of invited guests on
board the IWATE and on the following day these visiting officers
ore guests at a luncheon tendered them by the Japan Society
of Vancouver.
The popular interest in the visit of the Squadron with
its complement of more than fifteen hundr-d officers and sailors
was evidenced by the sightseeing and other arrangements that
had been made for the entertainment of the visitors and by the
large crowds that daily at all times thronged the docks along-
side the ships and visited the ships during the hours that
they were open to the public.
The successful outcome of this visit is apparent from
a VANCOUVER SUN editorial of April 6, as quoted below:
"OUR PACIFIC NEIGHBOR CALLS"
"As official representatives of the Japanese
people -- our great and good neighbors across the
Pacific - the warships YAKUMO and IWATE, Vice Admiral
Hyakutake, 'his officers and men, are exceedingly wel-
come in Vancouver.
"However much we may disagree with Japan's
present policy in Manchukuo, that disagreement does
not by one jot abate the warmth of our regarc for the
Japanese.people.
"This is a call of courtesy by a people who arc
the very personification of courtesy.
"And as we receive them, we remember with
gratitude the utmost graciousness with which the
Japanese have always greeted and treated our own
Canadian visitors in Japan.
"We remember, too, the part Japanese warshins
played in protecting this coast from German raiders
during the Great War.
"However

=== Page 60 === (confidence 0.97)
"However we may criticize Japanese policy, or
they ours, the Japanese nation is our good friend.
Vancouver and Canada are glad to be able to return
some of their many courtesies."
Pespectfully yours,
y F. Palmer,

put on the Pacific Coast, especially at San Francisco.
3
161
3
He said that this was an evidence of the really friendly
attitude of the American people toward Japan. I remarked
that I had on several occasions assured the Ambassador
that the American people entertain very friendly feelings
toward the Japanese people. He said that he realized
that, but that portions of the American press indulge
in a great deal of criticism of Japan and particularly
of the Japanese Army. I remarked that the whole world

1C

also had been gratified over the receipt of reports
which indicated that the reception had been cordial
and the officers and men of the Japanese Fleet were
being given and were enjoying a good time. The Ambas-

=== Page 72 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. /. to Despatch No./553 of June  1933.
AMERICAN LEGATION
Clipping from THE PANAMA AMERICAN of April 21, 1933
PANAMA, R. de P.
Rambling, Gambling and Publishing
By NELSON ROÚNSEVELL
To some it is a mystery why BRiTISH nationality seems to
be so much more deeply ingrained into its subjects than that of
other countries — our own, for instance.
To those who have traveled widely and lived long in foreign
lands the mystery is not so great. The answer being that even
in the most remote corners of the earth a BRITISHER, no mat-
ter how obscure or unknown individually, always has protection
of the BRITISH FLAG, and an injury, threat or injustice to one
or more of its minor subjects — anywhere in the world — is
immediately made the CONCERN OF THE EMPIRE.
The lengths to which Great Britain has recently gone in de-
fense of and in offering NATIONAL PROTECTION to her sub-
jects charged in Russia with sabotage, is typical. No other coun-
try in the world, if my observation of precedents be correct,
would take the daring action that Great Britain has to save those
relatively obscure workingmen involved in difficulties with 
powerful foreign government.
And that is why Britishers, the world over, are a bit more
cockey, more unconcerned, more SURE OF THEMSELVES than
the rest of us on foreign soil. They KNOW, and so does the rest
of the world, just how far Britain will go in defense of its most
humble subject against injustice and in preventing wrong being
done them.
We will now watch with interest the attitude of the United
States Government in the case of the two AMERICAN soldiers
arrested Wednesday in China, charged with "PHOTOGRAPH-
ING JAPANESE TROOP MOVEMENTS." But it will require
a long stretch of the imagination to picture the United States
going to the extreme in protective measures in defense of those
two soldiers that Great Britain has gone in the case of her sub-
jects charged with Sabotage in Russia. Let us hope, however.
that she DOES.
It is pertinent to remark in this connection that JAPS
HAVE BEEN PHOTOGRAPHING AMERICAN "TROOP
MOVEMENTS," FORTIFICATIONS AND NAVAL OPERA-
TIONS ON THE ISTHMUS OF PANAMA FOR A DECADE.
And I have yet to hear of one boing ARRESTED, slapped on the
wrist, or even told to "GET THE HELL OUT OF HERE."
On Sundays, holidays, and al kinds of days — seven days
a week, if you want to know the truth — JAPS — deep chested.
broad shouldered youths who diplay MILITARY TRAINING
in th: very step and gesture — roam the Canal Zone AT WILL
— S. CHING and PHOTOGRAPHING — and have been
sailing in fishing (?) boats, DAY AND NIGHT FOR TEN
YEARS over the waters of Panama Bay — and I have yet to 
hear of ANYBODY, even as much as saying out loud "WHAT IN
HELL ARE YOU FELLOWSDOING HERE?"
In recent years the back strests of Panama and Colon have
filled up — quietly and without y apparent notice being taken
of it — with JAP barber (?) shops — some of which don't cut
enough hair in a month to pay their tent.
Who has taken note of the surprising number of JAP shirt
(7) factories that have sprung up, almost over-night in Panama
end Colan? And, I esk you — who ever caught one of them SELL-
ING A.SHIRT?
I have personally strolled into several — merely out of idle
curiosity. Show cases filled with silk shirts, camouflaging an
obscure hole-in-the-wall. Two or three SEWING MACHINES
ranged around the wall - seated in front of which, with legs
indolently crossed, are been looking, watchful men of fine phy
sique who look to me more hke NAVAL and MILITARY officers
ease and then another, displaying obvious interest in SHIRTS
- the jape remain INDIFFERENT!

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2-
It those JAPS were actually dependent upon MAKING or
SELLING SHIRTS for a living and to pay rent, would they let
a prosperous lookng Gringo who apparently wanted a shirt, get
out of their store without at least TRYlNG to sell him one?
Not on your life. They would be BUTTONHOLING Grin-
goes on the sidewalks, just as the East Indians do on Front
Street and Central Avenue, and DRAGGING them inside in ef-
forts to make a SALE.
And so I have for some weeks strolled into and out of those
JAP SHIRT (?) SHOPS without having one question asked of
me and without having opportunity to ask them a question. And
I can show you JAP BARBER (?) SHOPS in Panama and Colon
that do not SHAVE enough customers in a week to pay their
light bills — and in which there are TWICE AS MANY BARB-
BERS (?) as there are BARBER CHAIRS.
And those are the birds who held up CHARLES and ANNE
LINDBERGH for days during the progress of their spectacular
and daring trans-Pacific flight — PIONEERING IN AVIATION
because they had a CAMERA in the plane and had flown OVER
Japanese fortification:.
And now those despoilers of LIBERTY and FREEDOM in
CHINA, who send SPIES to Panama by the hundred, arrest
couple of American soldiers for "PHOTOGRAPHING TROOP
MOVEMENTS!"
If the United States does not go to equal or greater lengths
in DEMANDING THE RELEASE and in giving NATIONAL
PROTECTION — in the fullest sen e and meaning of the word
— in behalf of those two American soldiers that Great Britain
has gone in defense of her subjects in Russia — then there is.
something wrong with AMERICA'S GUTS.
And if somebody does not soon arise OFFICIALLY and de-
mand to know what in hell so many JAPS — without visible
means of support or apparent reason or legitimate occupation —
ARE DOING ON THE ISTHMUS OF PANAMA — then THE
PANAMA AMERICAN will not only ASK thq question, but will
ANSWER it as well.
FISHERMEN? — BAH! — SHIRT MAKERS? — POOHI
BARBERS? — LIKE HELL THEY ARE!
And what kind of a SLEEPING POTION has been taken by
ur famous G-2 and our bonsted Naval Intelligence and our
widely advertised SECRET SERVICE that here — at the most
crucial vortex of America's NATIONAL DEFENSE — JAPS
who ARREST buck privates for PHOTOGRAPHING TROOP
MOVEMENTS IN CHINA — are permitted to organize and
operate in Panama the most complete and elaborate SPY SYS.
TEM that has ever functioned on foreign soil — WITHOUT
MOLESTATION, and, so far as is now apparent, WITHOUT
EFFECTIVE surveillance or interference.
And this JAP organization — this JAP ARMY OF OCCU
PATION (armed, as would appear, with razors, sewing needles
and fish hooks — and occupationed as fishermen, tailors and
barbers) is today TWICE AS NUMEROUS as officialdom has
anv knowledge or record of — and ten times as ALERT as the
average American might suppose — NUMEROUS ENOUGH
TO DESTROY THE CANAL IN ONE HOUR ANY NIGHT
THEY MAY CHOOSE TO DO SO — and this IN SPITE OF
ANY PRECAUTIONS our Generals and Admirals have yet taken.
Too preoccupied at Quarry Heights with determining how
to sell three-point-two beer and with usurping the authority of
CIVILIAN COURTS and white-washing JACK-LIGHT cases_to
pay any attention to a very REAL and threatening JAP MENACB
on the Isthmus.

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.99)

Clipping from THE PANAMA AMERICAN of April 25, 1933
AMERICAN LEGATION
PANAMA, R. de P.
Rambling Gambling
and Publishing
By Nelson Rounsevell
Alwaye a Ramblar. Fer Years a Gambler, Now a Publisèn
So far as I am able to ascertain, JAPANESE IN PANAMA
enjoy all the rights, freedom, opportunity and advantages of citi-
zens of the "MOST FAVORED NATIONS" and may go and
come at will with no more restrictions than North Americans or
white Europeans. Just WHY this "favored nation" preference is
shown JAPANESE, while Chinese, West Indians and dozeng
of other nationalities are on the RESTRICTED list as UN.
DESIKABLES is hard to understand.
So far as I am able to learn, no Japanese has ever invest-
ed a dollar in Panama real estate; has ever erected a building:
has ever promoted any industry that required greater equipmen
than a fishing boat, barber chair or sewing machine; not one
has ever paid a dollar of TAXES in Panama (except a very few
that have been made to pay a "head tax" for the upkeep of roads).
not one maintains a HOME in the sense of the word that any
self-respecting Panamanian would call a home; not one has ever
contributed in even a remote or indirect way toward the PRO.
GRESS, PROSPERITY OR UPLIFT OF PANAMA — yet they
enjoy all the "favored nation" benefits and privileges of the
country.
Japanese in Panama ignore practically all the regulations
affecting other foreigners and, by hook and by crook, manage
to avoid payment of all municipal and other taxes and fees to
which the rest of us foreigners are subjected. WHY? That is the
question that I am investigetin vand that, to clear its own skirtr
of suspicion of OFFICIAL CONNIVANCE WITH JAPANESI
ORGANIZATIONS, ev
rtment of the Panama Govern-
ment should at once in
And, in the mean t
these investigations are going
forward and while these rtling revelations are being made
by The Panama Ameri
es every American who has
the interests of his cou
a the safety of our National De.
fense at heart, to KEEr
EYES AND EARS OPEN — and
WATCH EVERY MQ
OF THOSE JAPS IN PANA-
MA AND COLON —
port immediately to the proper Mi-
litary, Naval, Police
plomatic authorities anything and
everything of a
nature and make available to
those whose busine
terests in the matter of National Defense on the Isthmus, all
possible information of the movements, conversations and acti-
vities of the Japanese in our midst.
In the matter of "REGISTRATION OF FOREIGNERS"
— a subject that has occupied Panama's official attention for
some years, and has caused other foreigners here no end of in-
convenience and expense — a recent check-up indicates that not
more than ONE-THIRD of the Japanese now on the Isthmus
are, or ever have been REGISTERED, and Panama's Foreign
Office today has no official record or knowledge of more than
one-half the Japanese subjects now in the Republic.
Up to and including the end of the year 1930, a total of 140
Japanese had recorded the required information in the Public
Registrar's office, such registration having taken place in the
following years:
1917, 2; 1918, 12; 1919, 1; 1920, 4; 1922, 1; 1925, 7; 1926,
14; 1928, 71; 1929, 12; 1930, 6.
Of those 140 Japanese subjects registered to the end of
1930, occupations given are as follows:
Barbere, 60; fishermen, 46; tailors, 14; merchants, 9; wait-
ers, 2; one each, farmer, photographer, artist, sailor, candy maker
and chauffeur; and three whose occupation is unregistered.

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
n Iwo things are notable in connection with this curious oc-
upational list. First, and which should be of greatest interest
to PANAMANIANS, the occupations of FISHERMEN and
BARBERS is in direct competition with PANAMANIAN LABOR
in fields that, were it not for Japanese competition, might be
predominated by Panamanians. Especially is this true of MARK-
ET FISHING, a profitable and permanent National Industry.
now almost exclusively monopolized by Japanese who spend not
one dime of their earnings in the Republic beyond the barest
necessities of life, which, in their case, are very meager and
even below the standard of living among Panamanian workin!-
men — as many as 20 bachelor Japanese fishermen living on
one floor of one building, employing not even a Panamanian
servant or cook.
The other notable feature of these two predominating oe-
cupations is the convenience with which they lend themselves
to Japanese ESPIONAGE. The BARBER, perhaps more than
any other person in any trade or calling, has opportunity during
every working hour to listen to and take note of an endless
amount of loose, unguarded conversation on the part of men
from every walk in life. Barber shops the world over are head-
quarters for gossip, idle talk, scandal and under -ground infor-
mation of all sorts, gathered from the conversations of careless,
thoughtless customers and loungers from morning till night.
In the special case of espiona connection with Panama
Canal defenses what gerater opporunity could have been offer-
ed Japanese Naval and Military spies on their visits to Panama
than that afforded by a large fleet of JAPANESE FISHING
BOATS plying the waters of Panama Bay at all hours of the
day and night with the greatest freedom and latitude?
In this respect, "the horse is perhaps already stolen, and
it is probably a bit late to talk about "locking the door" — but
there is still greater damage that might yet be done by theso
fishing boats, undoubtedly manned with crews of Japanese
Naval and Military experts and observers.
American Military and Naval officers are indeed lacking
in IMAGINATION if they cannot conceive of this FLEET OF
JAP FISHING BOATS IN PANAMA BAY constituting a very
real and ever-present MENACE to the safety of Panama Canal
defenses if not actually to the Canal itself.
And it is opportune for officialdom in the Republic of Pana-
ma to immediately investigate and inquire into WHY Japanes
foreigners have not ALL registered. Are the Japs immune to.
the Registration Law that all of us Gringoes and West Indians
have been forced to comply with?
Have the Jap stores, shops and hideouts paid the SIGN
TAX which has been requ
other public establishments
(including The Panama An
Have these hundreds o
panese been required to pay the
SALARY TAX which has bm sbitrarily imposed upon all the
rest of us wage slaves? O re they immune to that too?
Officialdom in Panama  quite alert in seeing to it that
Gringoes and West Indians do not escape any of those TAXES.
Why are they not equally alert in the pursuit of Japanese dollars?
How much import duty has been paid in the past five years
by those Japanese merchants (?) whose stores employ only
JAPANESE HELP? Why has not the 75 PERCENT PANA-
MANIAN LABOR LAW been enforced against them? Are they
immune to that too?
Chinese merchants have been forced to employ Panaman-
ian clerks — but try and find a Panamanian anywhere who is
drawing a clerk's or any other kind of a salary from a Japanese.
Sly, wiley, deceitful, close-mouthed, unobtrusive, keeping
entirely to themselves, this large and rapidly growing colony
of Japanese in Panama and Colon has apparently escaped the
notice of both American and Panamanian officialdom — includ-
ing the tax collectors  and but scant attention has been paid
to their real activities. But the time has now come to throw a
aearch light upon their espionage and their plots in an effort to
OPEN THE EYES OF OFFICIALDOM.

AMERICAN LEGATION
C11pping from THE PANAMA AM RICAN of April 28, 1933 PAN Ma. aoP.
Rambling, Gambling and Publishin
By NELSON ROUNSEVELL
Always A Rambler — Once A Gambler — Now A Publisher
Everybody who is ANYBODY in these days has a PUBL
RELATIONS COUNSEL — a "contact" man, some of them c
it. I have long felt the need of an individual of this type, a
especially so since I have been persona non grata in so many
the offices wherein repose bulky figures in the largest swiv
chairs on the Isthmus.
After considerable scouting around I finally decided up
etaining BARRISTER SUNDOWN, wellknown in twilight leg
circles, as my PERSONAL Public Relations Counsel, and 
will hereafter make all my official calls upon POWERS THA
BE — as well as some that BE NOT.
In this manner I will be informed daily (after dark,
course) of the reaction to my remarks and activities of promi
ent and important individuals at the various HEIGHTS her
abouts. Last night BARRISTER SUNDOWN met me by :
pointment in the shrubbery on the fortified side of Ancon H
and related to me so many REACTIONS that I already feel w
repaid for the salary advance (retainer fee they call it in leg
parlance) that he had demanded, lawyer fashion, (that's in pai
graph one of all Correspondence Courses) and I pass the fac
on to you, as is newspaper custom, gratis and for nothing — 
ress you are cheap enough to count the nickel you paid for th
paper — or did you BORROW IT?
BARRISTER SUNDOWN advised me right off the bat ar
right under the mango tree that I AM ALL WET in my dedu
tions, conclusions, presumptions and insinuations in connectic
with the JAPANESE QUESTION, and he advised me to get :
umbrella and a life preserver lest I drown in my own all-wetnes
In the first place, said BARRISTER SUNDOWN, I have
from a spokesman of a very high Army Official (although,
course, neither the official or the spokesman can be quote
that in PEACE TIME it is not the ARMY'S BUSINESS eith
to GUARD or to PROTECT the Panama Canal, its locks, dan
spillways, railroad bridges, pump stations, shops powder mag
zines or SECRET FILES.
"THAT, in peace time," declared Barrister, the SPOKE
MAN said, quoting the HIGH OFFICIAL, "is the GOVERNOR
BUSINESS and that is what he has the Canal Zone Police f
and if he don't PROTECT those things it is just TOO BAD, b
the army has no JURISDICTION or RESPONSIBILITY ov.
those purely civilian matters in peace time."
"WHAT A PITY," I remarked," that General Brown do
not hold that same opinion and follow that same policy in tl
matter of MANSLAUGHTER, HUNTING WITHOUT
LICENSE and WITH JACK LIGHTS."
BARRISTER SUNDOWN got on more familiar ground whe
he commenced to tell me about the position of the GOVERNO:
on that same subject. It is the Governor's contention, Barrister
informed me, that there are NO JAPS IN THE CANAL ZONE.
except those working in the Barber Shops in Military Posts, and
that, unfortunately, he has no JURISDICTION over barber
shops in Fort Randolph, Fort Davis and France Field. And, even
if he had, Sundown explained, how could a mere Colonel ever
suggest to a General that his Canal Zone Police should be given
authority to watch the army's BARBERS?

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
And the responsibility of Canal Zone Police, Barrister et-
plained, is only to preserve mango trees, prevent unreasonable
tooting of auto horns, see that the various chairs at police sta-
tions are occupied the required number of hours daily, and pre-
vent any violation of the Canal Zone Code (when and if said
CODE is ever adopted and made LAW) and that unless the
Japanese climb a mango tree, toot an auto horn, commit rape in
a tool shed, or bring 3.2-PLUS into the Canal Zone, the Horse-
back Police have no right to arrest them.
Furthermore, my Public Relations Private Contact Counsel
tried to convince me that I am completely mistaken about who
are the SPIES and who the SPIED UPON in the case of Japan-
ese on the Isthmus. Barrister Sundown says that he has it on
the very highest possible Military, Naval and Diplomatic autho-
rity that these Japanese here are really secret agents of the
United States and that the Buck Privates who get shaved by
them actually get Japanese Military secrets from the barbers.
Adroitly, Sundown explains, our Military and Naval intel-
ligence men and our Department of Justice agents on the Isth-
mus ascertain from the Jap fishermen, shirt makers and barbers
the very latest and most intimate plans of the Japanese Govern-
ment for the conquest of the Pacific. And my inadvertent pub-
lication of so much clap-trap about the Japs, it appears, is mak-
ing it very difficult for our own Intelligence Corps to get the
information they want from the Japs.
This, Barrister argued, explains how so many Japs appear
prosperous without any visible means of support — they are
living on the money the United States pays them for divulging
Japanese Military secrets. This being the case, I beg Sr. Kata-
lino Amaro's pardon for the unkind insinuation that he is work-
ing in the interest of his own government, whereas, if Barrister
Sundown is correctly informed, he is actually trying to help us
guard the Panama Canal.
Tonight I have arranged to meet Sundown by moonlight
under the fourth royal palm tree on the south side of Admiral
Irwin's house and he has promised then to explain the Navy's
viewpoint on the Japanese situation and present their alibi in
connection with the Mikado's fishing operations in Panama Bay.
I hope the Admiral has a better alibi than the General had.
But, anyway, as Barrister pointed out, there are no Jap barbers
at the SUB BASE.

WHY THE JAP SECRECY?
(By Winston Jay Lung)
If friendship is the art of getting what you
want from people, local Japs are friends of Pan-
ama.
In many ways the writer has shown during
the past week how tricky Nipponese cheat the Pan-
ama Goyernment of revenues compete unfairly with
Panamanian fishermen and barbers, and undersell
Isthmian merchants.
And though these aons of the "Bising King-
dom" take all, they never give anythingrin return.
Who ever heard of Jap investing in Panama
real estate or contributing to relief campaigns and
aiding in the celebration of national holidays
such as carnival?

=== Page 85 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Yesterday was the 32nd birthday of Emperor
Hirohito, who succeeded his father, Yoshihito, to
the Japanese throne on December 26, 1926. Jap bar-
ber shops and stores were closed, and Nipponese
gathered at their club to commemorate the occa-
sion. But this re-union was held behind closed
doors and no aliens or Panamanians were present.
Regular meetings are held by the Japanese As-
sociation at house No. 14, Calle Colon, with Kata-
lino Amaro presiding. But have they ever invited
a Panamanian—public employee or private indivi-
dual — to attend ene of these meetings?
Can anybody state when and where any of
these selfish sons of the "Son of Heaven" have ever
asked an outsider to sip a cup of tea with them?
Has the Japanese Consul to Panama ever in-
vited the consular or diplomatic corps to his head-
quarters for dinner or a glass of champagne — or
even ice water? Has he ever opened his doors to
Panamanian officials or Canal Zone functionaries?
If the activities of local Japs are on the level
and above board — as they would have us believe
— why do they move about with the greatest sec-
recy and in a manner that arouses suspicion in the
minds of all? And why do they regard persons of
other nationalities with the cautiousness of a wild
deer?
On the other hand, take the case of local Chin-
ese residents. They have invested thousands in real
estate, made regular contributions to relief drives
— public or private — and have participated, fin-
ancially and otherwise, in holiday celehrations and
civic enterprises.
And whenever Chinese celebrate, Panamanians,
Americans and others don their holiday clothes and
enjoy their hospitality. The Chinese Legation in
Panama City is open to friends of the Chinese Re-
public at all times, and quite often the Chinese Min-
ister entertains officials and friends at dinner.
The reason for the opposite policy of the two
people is obvious. Chinese have nothing to hide and
their activities are on a purely legitimate basis.
But the Japanese? Éach is part of Japan's ela-
borate espionage system and cannot afford to mix
with others through fear of detection.
If Jap residents are friends of Panama and are
carrying out only legitimate business, it is time for
them to establish this contention to the satisfaction
of all concerned

AM!
Clipping from EL PANAMA AMERI CA of My 2, 1933. .P.
EL "DUMPING" JAPONES EN PANAMA
La presencia de una ya numerosa colonia ja-
ponesa dentro de las fronteras panameñas, disemi-
nados sus miembros con prelación en las dos ciuda-
des principales y la Zona del Canal y dedicados a
oficios muy conocidos (pescadores, camiseros, pelu-
queros etc., etc.,) ha sido motivo de alarma de par-
te de algunos periodistas, entre ellos el presidente
de The Panama-American Publishing Company, se-
ñor Nelson Rounsevell.
Ha expresado el colega en sus numerosos ar-
tículos la sospecha de que los japoneses desarrollen
en Panamá un programa de espionaje en favor de
los intereses militares de su país y con detrimento
de los Estados Unidos y de Panamá. Y con él opi-
nan muchas personas que tienen sus motivos para
mantener tal criterio. No son pocas las personas
que recuerdan la sorpresa que alemanes, aparente-
mente inocentes e inofensivos, dieron a la Francia
al declararse la guerra mundial al haber aprovecha-
do su permanencia en este último país para acopiar
datos y construir bases o cureñas para emplazar
cañones poderosos en sitios en donde jamás se pen-
só que guardaran elementos de tanto valor para
los alemanes.
Nosotros no deseamos hacer incursión en el te-
rreno de la especulación del espionaje japonés en
Panamá .Creemos de nuestro deber dar preferen-
cia a otra faz del que puede denominarse ya entre
nosotros el problema japonés. Esta faz es la incur-
sión comercial del Japón en Panamá que cada día to-
ma mayor incremento
El gobierno japonés por medio de casas comer-
cialcs de aparente explotación privada, está ejer-
ciendo en Panamá un verdadero "dumping" noto-
riamente perjudicial para nuestro país. Las llama-
das "casas japonesas", establecidás tanto en esta
capital como en Colón, desarrollan una desleal com-
petencia contra el comercio panameño. Además de
la competencia de los comisariatos. de la Zona del
Canal, tenemos ahora las "casas japonesas" que se
presentan en condiciones de realizar artículos a
precios cincuenta y hasta setenta y cinco por cien-
to más bajos que los corrientes.
Es cosa de apariencia inexplicable que tales es-
tablecimientos puedan mantener escalas de precios
tan reducidas, pero cuando se llega al conocimiento
de que los jornales son sumamente bajos en el Ja-
pón y que en favor de la expansión comercial, en el
mundo el gobierno del Mikado reconoce y paga a los
productores la diferencia que resulte entre el precio
de costo y el de venta a los exportadores, siempre
que este último precio sea más bajo que el primero,
hay que convenir que Panamá está siendo víctima
de un proyecto fantástico creado por la mente egoís-
ta de los japoneses.

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
El público consumidor se cree beneficiado con
la existencia de las "casas japonesas" porque paga
precios bajos. Pero si nos detenemos a ahondar pa-
ra descubrir las ra'ces del árbol económico de Pa-
namá, veremos de modo claro e irrebatible que el
aparente beneficio de tales consumidores se tradu-
ce en un no itivo perjuicio para el erario público.
En efecto. En el renglón de entradas del Fis-
co la partida más jugosa es el impuesto de intróduc-
ción, y qe el volumen de éste depende no solamen-
te de la contided de artículos que arribe al país si-
no también del valor de los mismos. De ahí que si
las "cases jnone sus" traen artículos liquidados a
prei s ri l'e los, la entrada que perciba el Estado
por d rechos de aduana resultará también ridícula.
Y pudiendo estas casas imponer, por las maniobras
del gohierno japonér, fuerte competencia a los otros
almacenes no prccisa un gran esfuerzo de imagina-
ción para convenir que estos últimos marcharían
al fraceso y a la eliminación. En ese camino no tar-
da el día en que en Panamá tengamos solamente
"casas japonesas" importadoras de artículos depre-
ciados y que pagan como impuesto de importación
sumas pequeñísimas. Con el correr de los días ha-
brá desanarecido la principal entrada de nuestro ré-
gimen tributario.
Para darse cuenta de la magnitud y la trascen-
dencia de este "dumping" japonés precisa determi-
nar lo que este inmigrante significa en el plano de
las actividades de la vida diaria. El japonés hace
una vida perfectamente aislada, no tiene propen-
sión por los esparcimientos ni ningún lugar le con-
vida. De ahí que constituya sólo un elemento explo-
tador y jamás un consumidor. Aquí en Panamá todo
el mundo sabe que el japonés se impone como gas-
tos solamente el cuarto que habita y la comida que
in iere. No propende, pues, al desarrollo de otras
ad ividades. Por otro lado, si toma empleado a al-
gún hijo del país, después de esclavizarle le ofrece
una miseria de B.7.50 y B.10.00 al mes. En esta for-
ma coopera al macabro plan de expansión comercial
de su patria.
Panamá debe abrir los ojos ante esta evidencia
del "dumping" japonés en Panamá.

Panamanian Interest
In Japanese Menace
Panama, May 3
Editor Panama American:
Wih regard to Mr. Rounse-
vell (pese of possible Japan-
emg ver Parama and the
Catnt we Pomamanians cannot
reean aloot and unconcerned
inrsmtch as we would also suf-
fer fron what might occur if
we continue with eyes shut in
front of possibilities that imay
take place. It is evident that
Japan has de ermined to be-
come mistress of the Pacific
Occan at any cost. The Japan-
ese are imbued wi'h a spirit of
sacut c  r count: y tha could
imoel them to any lengum.
Mr. Rounsevell is tied up with
our native Jand as much a: wo
ourselves are; he is bound up in
oneness with us by the invisible
bouris of the deepest affection.
Here among us he has put up
his tent definitely. and his tent-
pole has grown root; in our soil
iis firm as those of the boab
tree, the first tree to grow. in
Africin soil, as soon
world became habitable.
Mr.
Rounse etl is for us Panaman-
inns a vetehful spirit. even
thou h he has hit us somewhat
hard in polities. And as he has
given us proof of loving this
land. his present fears should
deserve our best consideration.
Especially when, should what
he fears ever happen, the locks
of Pedro Miguel and Miraflores
would give us, in case they were
to burst, a shower of such force
that we would tell the story of
it in Davy Jones' locker. And
when low tide came, it would
be too late.
Rounsevell is not goofy in the
head but firm along the path of
reason. A Japanese airplane
rising from anywhere could
well make corn-meal of the Pa-
nama Canal. And if this is pos-
sible, having the enemy right at
home among us should make us
sensitive to all kinds of risks.
Is it possible to believe that
there being so much fish in
Japanese seas and greater fa-
cilities there for catching them,
the honorable Japanese fisher-
men that we have with us
should have come all the way
over thousands of miles just to
catch our Tamborile, Congos
and Toadfish?
We must all be on the alert,
Panamanians as well as grin-
gos, and, under the boughs of a
better understanding of our
common danger, we should fra-
ternally embrace one another.
Alberto V. de Yeaza.

PANAMA. R de P
TWO JAPANESE
GIVE PROOF OF
REGISTRATION
Letter To Panama Am-
erican Encloses Cer-
tificates
Two of the 122
Japanese
whose names were published in
yesterday's Panama American
are registered, according to
proof obtained yesterday from
Yoshito Sadairo, legal repre-
sontative of the Casa Japonesa.
In a letter to the Panama A-
merican, Sadairo enclosed resi-
dence certificates of these two
sons of the Mikado as well as
certificates of four others
whose names were not included
in the list.
The letter from Sadairo fol-
lows:
Panama, May 5, 1933
Editor The Panama American,
City.
Sir:
In today's issue of your paper
there is printed a list of Japan-
ese subjects charged with not
having fulfilled the legal re-
quirements concerning residence
of foreigners whose immigra-
tion is allowed and who live in
this country.
In that list there appear the
names of KATSUO IZUKA. MO-
TOJI KUDO, TADAO SATO, SE-
BURO TAKAHASHI, SUNJI
SHUNDO and TAKEO HASHI-
MOTO, employees of this firm,
and as the information regard-
ing them is incorrect (these
men being duly registered as
domiciled residents in the pro-
per bureau and having filled all
legal requirements) I beg you to
correct your information with
regard to them.
To assure you of my statement
I am sending you the men's re-
sidence certificates, which you
will please return as soon as you
have examined them.
Thanking you for the atten-
tion which you may give this re-
quest, I remain, etc.
(S) Yoshito Sadairo,
Legal Representative of the
Japanese House.

=== Page 102 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No./. to Despatch No. /5 s3: of June 70. 1933.
AMERICAN LEGATION
JAPANESE IN RUMBLE SEAT
PANAMA
AMERICAN
NR ADMITS IT'S KNOCKED HIM FLAT

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.99)
PANAMA, R. dθP.
Rambling, Gambling and Publishing
By NELSON ROUNSEVELL
Nelson Rounsevell,
Panama American:
Uncle Sam, the modern "Rip Van Winkle" snores on while
his antiquated armament lies at his side, utterly useless. Can't
he be awakened by some method other than war? Can't the
gentlemen (?) up at Quarry Heights take some action in regards
to Japanese activities in the Canal Zone? American tax payers
are depending upon their intelligence and action to protect the
Panama Canal from any possible danger, but as far as anyone
around here knows, there hasn't been a single investigation nor
any concern about the shady activities of local Jap residents.
For the past week or so your paper has had the guts to call
Quarry Heights' attention to the danger surrounding us. You
have told about Jap fishermen roaming our harbers at all hours
of the night. You told us about open-eared, close-lipped barbers
in our own Army posts. You have told Quarry Heights all that
they had to know and yet nothing has been done about it.
What's wrong up there? Are they too proud to take preventive
measures because the Panama American brought it to their at-
tention? Is our intelligence department too dumb to cope with
the local Jap situation?
Americans despise war. They had their bellies filled with
poisonous gasses, shrapnel and bullets years ago. The American
tax payer sends men to West Point, and Annapolis, educates
them, and assigns them to responsible positions to Jook after
American rights and interests. The American public is depend-
ing on General Brown and his staff to protect the Panama Canal
Then why don't they do it?
Even with the 15 percent cut, sad to relate, the majority of.
them are not earning the tax payers hard earned cash. It's piti-
ful indeed. While resenting George Bernard Shaw's remark that
Americans are deaf. dumb and blind, nevertheless it is a fact.
Quarry Heights could well forget jack lights, hunting passes and
3.2 for a while and concentrate on our greatest local problem
which is rapidly becoming dangerous.
What about Panama? The Panama Canal is their very life
blood, yet they politely ignore the invasion of Panamanian ter
ritory by'non-restricted, non-tax paying, job grafting, spying Japs.
They are too busy putting up one way street signs and pinching
drunks to realize the seriousness of this invasion: They are play-
ing with fire and burned fingers will be the result unless action
is taken.
Time will tell and it will be written in blood unless both
Quarry Heights and Panama open their eyes soon.
The Jap in the Rumble Seat leaves but one impression. He's
a nut. Regardless of whether he be Japanese, American or Pana-
manian. No intelligent citizen of either country would cast in-
sults at another country's national anthem, especially when there
are strained relations existing between the two major powers
involved. It is hard to believe thas a citizen of either country
who had any horse sense at all would write such an article for
publication.
Americans want peace and harmony with other nations and
as long as Japan or any other power keeps off of our toes we will
respect them with all respect due them. However when it is
proven that Japan or any other dountry is secretly exploring our
harbors and fortifications it's high time that preventive measures
be taken. The individual can do nothing but protest. It's.up to
the men who are paid for this work to prevent the labor of years
being destroyed or captured by any foreign power at will.
Captain Smith's article in Liberty states the facts. The staff
at Quarry Heights should study that lowly captain's article, may-
be it would help. The tax payers are keeping the wrinkles out of
their bellies, so why shouldn't they in turn keep the wrinkles out
of the tax payer's bellies in the years to eome? In unity there
isstrenth,
Here's hoping we'll sodh see
An Ameriean in the Driver's Seat.

=== Page 104 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. /. to Despatch No./3-33 of June 2J.1933.
AMERICAN LEGATION
Clipping from THE PANAMA AMERICAN of May 8, 1933.
PANAMAHP
"We may come to that," the President answers without dif-
fidence. "Certainly we reached the human limit in the reduc-
tion of salaries and the lopping off of Government jobs."
A good many other topics are touched upon. One of them
is of special interest. I ask the President his opinion of the
Japanese menace that the Panama American is alarmedly
pointing to.
"It has aroused interest," the President replies. "As regards
myself personally or my Government, we have not yet had time
either to consider the facts presented or to form a definite opi-
nion on the matter. However, should there be such a menace
as resulting frora unmistakable proofs of espionage on the
part of any nation, the course that Panama must take is clear.
Not only in defense of her own interests so vitally linked with
the interests of the Canal, nor alone from having honestly to
fulfill contractual obligations towards the United States, but
from a sense of that duty that we owe to our neighbors. We
would fail in a moral obligation if we were found laggard in
looking out for what is of great importance to those who amic-
ably live side by side with us."

To M1. Nelson Rounsevell

your

more personal

Enclosure No. /. to Despatch No. /3s3. of May 20. 1933.
AMERICAN LEGATION
Rambling, Gambling and Publishing
By NELSON ROUNSEVELL
Always A Rambler — Once A Gambler — Now A Publisher
Day after tomorrow two Japanese Naval ships will arrivo
in Balboa and for two weeks more than 1,500 officers and men
of the Japanese Navy will rub elbows with the cosmopolitan
populace at the World's Crossroads.
High ranking Japanese officers will be banqueted and feted
by equally high ranking personnel of the American Army and
Navy, by foreign diplomats and by Panamanian officials. Higher-
ups of the local Japanese colony will for a fortnight shine in the
resplendant reflection of tho glory of the Mikado's Navy and
will make the most of every opportunity to have themselves taken
seriously in this exchange of perfunctory courtesies.
It is my purpose also that the affair be taken SERIOUSLY.
The Chinese are now taking Japan seriously — and it is high
time that we do too. The League of Nations is taking Japan so
seriously that it maintains a discreet silence while Japan thumbs
her nose at its mandates and tosses its notes into the intetna-
tional waste basket. And it is time that Panama and the United
States take seriously the obvious glances that Japan has long
been casting in the direction of our CANAL.
In the serious consideration of thopanese, it is important
first to understand that PATRIOTISMrules the Japanese to an
almost fanatical degree and that LOVE OF THEIR GOUNTRY
is so bound up withheir religion — the Emperor being regarded
as an incarnate god that to them DEATH in theweervice of
country is equivalent to a direct Heavenly passport
This presents a matter of psychology difficult fo
Vest-
ern mind to comprehend. The idea of associating d the
Hereafter is past ouinderstanding. We cannot
on earth is DEATH — IN THE MIKADO'SSEVICE.
The popularity of HARA-KIRI — a form sui de by self-
disembowelment — illustrates the workin
Nippo-
nese mind in this connectoin. For centuti  was compul-
thinkable crime upon themselves in atonemen for arousing the
slightest displeasure or dis proval of the
o.
The thing was done aceording to a pre
d ritual in pub-
lic. The suicide, frequently surrounded b riends and high
officials, plunged a dagger into his tomach mow the waist on
the left side, drew it slowly acrosstythe right, followed by an
upward slash.
While his life's blood spurted
his bowels flowed in a sickenin
the ground, and the
nearly severed body slumped l
took flight to the highest realme apanese Heaven, reserved
especially for those who DIED FOR THE EMPEROR — OR
AT HIS COMMAND.
Hara-Kiri is no longer compulsory in Japan. But the signi-
ficant hold that it still has upon the superstition and imagina-
tion of the Japanese people is evidenced by the fact that some
1,500 hara-kiris still take place annually in Japan — PURELY
AS A VOLUNTARY GESTURE, It was not long since that
Jap disembowelled himself in front of an American Consulate —
in Heavenly Protest to some met or declaration on the part of
the American Government.

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Keep this national trait of the Japanese people and this
Divine (?) tenet of Japanese religion and this incomparable ele
ment of Japanese patriotism in mind while you are assaying
the caliber of the men in Japanese uniform who will crowd our
clubs and grace our banquet tables and exchange courteous
bows and empty phrases with our Generals, Admirals, Diplomat
and Officials during the ensuing fortnight.
And don't let any Rumble Seat wise-cracks persuade you
that it is not a SERIOUS PEOPLE that we are to deal with and
that it is not a SERIOUS SITUATION that we face.
And don't for one moment doubt that every last one of tlie
Japanese now on the Isthmus — from the most aristocratic
merchant on down to the most lowly barber and fisherman —
would GLORY IN THE OPPORTUNITY TO GIVE HIS LIFE
in the carrying out of any diabolical plan for the destruction or
capture of the Canal or the annihilation of the American and
Panamanian population of the Isthmus that the present MILI-
TARY POWER in Japan might desire or devise.
And don't for a single second kid yourselves into the idea
that Japan will be one bit more considerate of the lives, feel-
ings or rights of Spigs, Gringoes or Chombos than she has of
the Chinks — we all look alike to the Japs. Our Isthmus is not on
bit less desirable to Japan or less necessary to her plans for
the CONTROL OF THE PACIFIC than is Manchuria.
And while you view our marvelous (?) so-called PANAMA
CANAL DEFENSES — which these same Japs have spied upon,
photographed, mapped and laughed at for more than a decade
— and while you view with awe the nightly play of search lights
glittering heavenward and focussed on airplanes in the sky —
seemingly, and purposely, intended to convince you that THE
PANAMA CANAL IS SAFE AND AMPLY GUARDED —
don't forget that any fifty of the Japanese naval and military
experts NOW ON THE ISTHMUS can go out any night in the
week carrying only a lunch-box full of nitro glycerine each and
blow up in the space of fen minutes EVERY IMPORTANT
ELEMENT OF THE PANAMA CANAL — including its un-
guarded power stations, water works, shops and railroad bridges.
And while the search lights play and our airplanes flit about
the skies in mimic battle formation and our big guns fire prac-
tice shots — don't forget to keep your eyes focused upon the
obscure barbers, (?) fishermen (?) and shirt-makers (?) who
long have been in our midst — and REMEMBER that every
st one of them would be only too happy at a moments notice
at word from th Emperor or in service of the Emperor —
blow himself to Kingdom Come — straight into the highest
in Japanese Heaven — along with the locks, docks, bridges.
ates and power plants of the Panama Canal — whenever
o may serve the purposes of Japan in her present MILI
progress toward SUPREMACY IN THE PACIFIC, do
mination of WORLD TRADE and defiance of WORLD POW.
ERS — to which Manchuria is but the stepping stone.
Read too, in this connection, an article "JAP PILOTS RIDE
TO DEATH" in the May issue of "Modern Mechanix and In-
ventions" in which is described and illustrated how Japanese air-
bombs are piloted to their mark by a human being inside the
bomb who goes to hi certain death — AND TO HEAVEN —
as bomb and pilot accomplish their mission of DEATH AND
DESTRUCTION.
Rumors of the flying-bomb  th-ride have come out of
the Japanese-Chinese conflict  stories that are UNBELIEV.
ABLE at first thought, but not when seen in the light of a peopl
fired by the same intense patriotie influence that inspires HARA
KIRI.
Americans and Panamanians alike must keep their eye
and ears open and their senses slert. Decide for yourselves wha
all the barbers, fishermen and shirt-makers are here for an
watch their every move - while you at the same time watcl.
the obvious trend of world affairs — DRIFTING — MORI
CERTAINLY — DAY BY DAY — TOWARDS WAR. WAR
By whom? With whom? When?
Heaven only can answer and Time only will tell. But they
are ALL getting ready — INCLUDING THE UNITED STATES
AND JAPAN — sad, but true, that is all too evident. The pre-
ent semeume for national sicatas.

auled. General Brown, accom-
panied by his aides, left cards
and regrets that the Adm. was
not aboard. Aithough the guard

al Brown's arrival, he was met

by a smiling and ever-courteous

Early
yesterday afternoon,
Governor Schley returned the
Admiral's visit
aboardthe
Iwate, after wnich the Admiral
returned General Brown's call.
At 3:30 o'clock Minister Arose-
mena returned Admiral Hyaku-
take's visit.
During the morning and while
Admiral Hyakutake was visiting
in the Canal Zone and Panama,
Captain Harry L. Pence, com-
mander of the U.S.S. Mempnis,
paid his official call to Admiral
Hyakutake.
The
Admiral's
chief-of-staff returned the call
yesterday afternoon, arter which
the wardrobe officers of the
Memphis were sent to call on
the wardrobe officers of the
Iwate.
The day's formalities ended
last night at the Union Club,
when Consul Wakabayashi en-
tertained the visiting officers
and local officials and their
wives and guests at a banquet.
Following the dinner, the party
joined the dancing held at the
Union Club regularly on Satur-
day nights.
A dinner and a luncheon for
the Admiral and his staff are
scheduled for today. At 1 p.m.,
Admiral Woodward will be host
at luncheon aboard the Mem-
phis, and Admiral Irwin will en-
tertain the visitors at dinner at
his home this evening. The re-
ception aboard the Iwate will be
held Thursday afternoon from
4 to 6 o'clock.
All in all, yesterday's ceremo-
nies were gala and at the same
time serious and entertaining.
In addition to the local Japan-
ese welcomers, hundreds of Pa-
nama and Canal Zone residents
lined the dock as the dark grey
cruisers came alongside, and
many of them, too, joined in the
welcome given the visitors by
the Panama Japanese colony.

Enclosure No./. to Despatch No./555. of June 20.1933.
AMERICAN LEGATIO
Clipping from THE PANAMA AMERICAN of May 16, 1933. PANAMA. R. doP.
Claims Japan
Is Menace To
World Peace
Secret a r y of Chinese
Legation Lectures
At Balboa High
"Japan — a menace to world
peace," was the topic of an ad-
dress delivered before Balboa
High School students yesterday
morning by Colonel Jonyor C.
Liao, secretary of the Chinese
Legation in Panama.
Commenting on the Nippon-
ese invasion of China, Colonel
Liao asserted it was not due
to over-population in Japan
nor lack of natural resources.
He said the Japanese were
suffering from a "complex
psychosis," that they feel the
world is against them and that
they are in a dangerous posi-
tion.
As a result, Colonel Liao said,
Japan has assumed an arro-
gant attitude, and with a war
machine responsible to nobody,
is a real menace to her neigh-
bors — Chiha and the United
States.

PERSIFLAGE
Free Talk About a Banquet
That was. they say, a swell dinner given at the Union Club
the other evening in honor of the Japanese high officers in
command of the warships of His Imperial Majesty of Japan
visiting us. Being perfectly free to speak my mind on any sub-
ject I wish, I will now embark on a few considerations regard-
ing that dinner and all other functions, of the sort. They are
rawest bunk that I know of we the common people have to put
up with.
It's stinking when diplomats get together. They are sup-
posed to be liars, anyway. Their blah blah is all by code and
you know beforehand just what they are going to say. They
will exchange the most polite and high sounding piffle and
then communicate with their office   home counseling mur-
der. Even at that. present day regular diplomats are but de-
generates. The great masters of the art were the Florentines
of Macchiavelli's time, and Madchtavelli, the greatest of them
all and an honest man besides,once and for all time exposed
diplomacy so that whoever has an ounce of real brains cannot
be bamboozled by that sort of stuff
And the proof of what I say is his, that when a man in
the diplomatic service does work that stands out as significant
and worthwhile, it is immedfately seen that he has broken all
rules of diplomacy and has fust been hunman. There is a dip-
lomat of that sort in Panama, lu man" Davis. In Morrow
the United States had another fellow of that caliber. Boys and
girls, a penny for Roy Tasco's thoughts at the festivities in
honor of our friends the Japs!
Well, a diplomat has a lot of nasty pills to swallow. But
think of those who are not diplomats sticking their noses into
that sort of mess. Think of the educators.
Get any one of those educators and buttonhole him and
ask him whether he is for war continuing its merry dance on
the face of the planet or for peace to be striven for, and the
man will declare himself for peaoe Then ask him, as if you
were Socrates cross examining some one, whether he believe
that peace should be established by force or by moral suasion,
and he will go great lengths to extol morality. But that same
kind of guy will fail you when the time comes for him to take
a definite moral attitude and to make a people gone mad with
war understand that they do not enjoy and cannot enjoy the
respect of the world.
What were educators doing at the banquet in honor of the
Japanese naval officers?
What kind of a lesson is that for the youth whom they are
supposed to be guiding towards a higher plane of morality?
One of the Japanese made a clever speech. Greatly grati-
fied by so many of the leading people of a lot of civilized na-
tions being gathered to feast the Japanese naval officers, he
stated that that sort of thing forecast a bright future for Japan.
Sure! If Japan can murder men, women and children in
China by the thousands; if she can destroy Chinese homes by
the cityful in the most shocking exhibition of barbarity that
the modern world has seen; if she can flaunt every decent feel-
ing that humanity has painfully groped upwards to attain, and
then have her representatives, her soldiers, sit with fine ladies
and be toasted in champagne, Japan is fortunate and her
spokesman at that banquet had reason aplenty to feel happy.
But what about the educators that were there?
Would you feast with the monsters that assassinated Lind-
bergh's child? Would you rub knees under the table cloth with
Al Capone's gangsters? Is not Japan morally in the same class
with those other criminals named?
I am a pacifist. I have less use for warlords and war vas-
sals of any sort than for diplomats. I would not, yet, have any
one of them done violence to. But abolish their class, yes. And
take from their class while it exists every prestige, most as-
suredly. And certainly I, as an educator, would be the last to
make such a farce of education as to lend my presence in order
to do honor to men whose profession is to murder, and espec-
jally when those men represent a country that is at present
the embodiment of aggressive warfare.
PERSILES,

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. /. to Despatch No./555. of June 20. 1933.
AMERICAN LEGATION
Clipping from THE RANAMA AMERICAN of May 16, 1933. PANAMA, R. doP.
Rambling, Gambling and Publishing
By NELSON ROUNSEVELL
Always A Rambler — Once A Gambler — Now A Publisher
Not long since, the Peruvian Cruiser "Almirante Grau." was
denied the ordinary courtesies of the port at Balboa, and was
compelled to move on within 24 hours without receiving the re-
pairs, fuel and provisions of which she was reputed to be des-
perately in need.
From unofficial sources it is understood that this denial of
Peru's request was the direct result of "orders from Washingtone"
And it has been assumed that Washington's attitude, and the
acquiescent and implied, if not directly expressed approval of
Panama was based on the notorious fact that Peru is "at war"
with Colombia.
In this attitude, both Panama and the United States are in
full accord with the openly expressed policy of the League of Na-
tions and definitely in support of its precepts.
It is not my purpose to criticise, even by inference, the policy
pursued by either Panama or the United States in turning a cold
shoulder upon Peru and in inviting her battle ships to be on their
way without repairs or supplies except such as they were able to
pick up by purchase through private individuals during the brief
24 hours the vessels were permitted to remain in port here. On
the contrary, Iendorse that policy and approve that attitude.
But it is my purpose to point out the entirely different at
titude pursued in the case of the two Japanese battle cruisers
now in port at Balhoa, and to direct attention to the extreme cour-
tesy with which the officers and personnel of Japan's fighting
ships are received by the very same officials who recently frown-
ed upon the Peruvians because of Leticia.
Lest it be first pointed out by some expert in the technicali-
ties of International Law, let me hasten to admit that the Peru-
vian vessels were obviously "on a hostile mission." Then let me
enquire who knows, beyond the Japanese'own word for it, that
the "Iwate" and the "Yakumo" are on a peaceful mission? Who
knows whither they are bound, or how long it may be before
those very ships may be shelling Chinese ports and those "Cadets"
will be disembarking with fixed bayonets to seize and hold still
more Chinese territory and butcher still more of China's people?
At least, those Japanese cruisers are commanded by naval
officers on the active list and the cadets are, more than likely.
serving their final training before actually engaging in warfare
against China — or, perhaps, some other nation a good deal closer
home to all of us.
The cruel, unjustified warfare that Japan has for months
and months been waging against China and on Chinese territory
is of such a brutal, aggressive and unprovoked a character, and
is so obviously waged with the sole purpose of the acquisition of
Chinese territory for Japan, as to have provoked the open protest
of the League of Nations and to arouse the openly expressed dis-
approval and indignation of the entire civilized world.
The condemnation of Japan from the Society of Nations has
been in terms much more severe than any language yet addressed
to Peru. And Japan's fiendish slaughter of Chinese, shedding
their blood in defense of their own native soil, has aroused the
unanimous condemnation of the whole world.

=== Page 127 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
Therefore, I contend. the lavish welcome to the Japanese
cruisers on the part of official Panama and United States that
has been witnessed since last Saturday can be construed as noth-
ing short of official approval on the part of those governments of
Japan's military invasion of China and tie ruthless slaughter of
the Chinese people.
On the other hand, the attitude of Panama and the United
States as expressed in the ceremonials given since last Safurday
in "honor" of the Japanese visitors, may easily be construed by
the numerous Chinese colony on the Isthmus as one of disdain
for their feelings and of contempt for their country's sufferings —
thus royally do they welcome China's cruel foe.
Still more, it may even he interpreted as flaunting the very
poignant feelings of outraged civilized humanity everywhere —
in this hypocritical kow-towing to the fighting units of a nation
OTYHO
Rather than as an outright protest against tho arrtesies now
being extended to the Japanese Government, ms represented in
the person of Vice Admiral Hyakutake, I now expres the heart-
felt hope that no dfense has been meant to the Chinese colony
on the Isthmus on the part of those high Panamanian, American
and Diplomatic officials who have broken bread, sipped wine,
danced and exchanged meaningless hothings with the Japanese
visltors.
And if the recent discriminations against the "Almirante
"Grau" seem to have been reversed in the case of the Japanese
war vessels, this has bel in obedience to exigencies beyond
the comprehension of the rank and file of Americans and Pana-
manians on the Isthmus, and I believe I express the sentiments
of that rank and file when I give voice to the hope that it is not,
as might be inferred, because both Panama and the United States
as well as the Diplomatic Corps in Panama hesitate to offend
so powerful a nation as Japan, while not caring a damn whether
they offend a bankrupt, revolution torn country like Peru or not.
In these words I speak but for myself — solely as but ONE
American — unafraid to hurl in the faces of our (to me) offen-
sive visitors the truth of my own opinion of their deeds and in-
tentions in the long continued, brutal and unjustified attack
against China.
Let him who will HISS and voice his disapproval of these
rude sentiments — the day will come, all too soon, I fear, when
China will not be alone in the sad necessity of shedding her blood
in defense of homeland and ideals against the war-mad, aggres-
sion-bent, expansion-determined JAPANESE!

Why The Kow-Towing
Mr. Nelson Rounsevell.
Panama American.
The following letter is ad-
dressed to you, for your views.
You being the only man to my
knowledge in our local Newspa-
perdom, that has the guts to
express opinions in a fearless
manner. no matter who it hits.
and no matter who it praises on
matters of civic interest.
We, the common people, are
wondering at this time. Why so
much honor is being bestowed
on the Nipponese visiting Squa-
dron and its officials, in con-
trast to the former visit of this
Naval Squadron?
We remember that two years
ago, this same Squadron visited
these waters, and that they re-
ceived the usual Naval honors
only, honors that Naval eti-
quette demands, the Naval Ca-
dets under training int
that
former Squadron are at pres-
ent attached to the Japanese
Fleetthat 4s watching our U. S.
Fleet in the Pacific. At that
time Japan was at peace with
the World, at this period Japan
defies the whole World.
We have the spectacle of our
Military, Naval, and other of-
ficials falling all over themselves
in kow-towing to the little Nip-
ponese. Why is this ?
If the former visit was done
according to Hoyle, why all the
fuss and feathers that are in
evidence at this visit? Are they
white feathers
