Enclosure No. /. to Despatch No.)s-ss~. of June 0.1933.
AMERICAON
Clipping from THE PANAMA AMERICAN of May 17, 1933.
PANAMA R deP.
Rambling, Gambling and Publishing
By NELSON ROUNSEVELL
Always A Rambler — Once A Gambler — Now A Publisher
Arriving in Panama last Septemher, was Mr. T. TAHARA,
who since then has posed as a traveling representative of a Jap-
anese Steamship Company, and who for eight months has lived
in luxury, style and ease at the hotels TIVOLI and WASHING.
TON.
The gross cargo and passenger business of his Isthmian
steamship agency has not amounted to enough in those eight
months to pay his hotel bills.
Mr. Tahara has lived most of the time at the Washington
in close and convenient proximity to the steamship offices of
Boyd Bros. Ine. in Cristobal, local agents for the OSAKA SHO.
SEN KAISHA steamship line which he claims to represent and
via which his confidential messages and reports have gone to
Japan.
Mr. Tahara has long been suspected of being a COMMIS.
SIONED OFFICER IN THE JAPANESE NAVY, on active ser-
vice in the intelligence department and his activities for some
weeks have been under the vigilant watchfulness of Panama
American scouts and investigators.
Those suspicions were fully borne out when Mr. Taharm
crossed the Isthmus on the day before the arrival of the Imperial
Japanese Cruisers Iwate and Yakumo, when he made reservations
at Hotel Tivoli for himself and VICE ADMIRAL HYKUTAKE
and their Aides.
Since the arrival of the two Japanese cruisers Mr. Tahara
has been paid all the honor and deference due to a SUPERIOR
BEING and he very obviously outranks, in power and importance,
the Vice Admiral himself. It is Mr. Tahara, and not the Vice
Admiral, who gives the final "Yes" or "No" upon important
questions of policy and diplomacy in their Isthmian program
and arrangements.
It is Mr. Tahara, steamship agent (BAH!) who made ar-
rangements for and who was in complete charge of the transit
of the Canal yesterday on the Favorita which carried Japanese
officers and cadets from Balboa to Gatun enroute to Coco Solo
Naval Base.
Mr. T. TAHARA is the CHIEF of those Japanese SPIES
on the Isthmus about whom I have been talking in recent weeks
who live here WITHOUT VISIBLE MEANS OF SUPPORT,
without apparent reason or excuse for being here, who are in
surreptitious contact with American Naval and Army sources of
information and whose obvious business here is to keep Japanese
Naval and Military authorities posted on every minute detail
and movement of AMERICAN DEFENSE ON THE ISTHMUS
OF PANAMA.
It is Mr. T. TAHARA who outranks and who commands all
the minor spies and secret agents which have so long been ca-
mouflaged in BARBER SHOPS, FISHING BOATS and SHIRT
FACTORIES in Panama and Colon, and his surreptitious contacts
with that whole corps of barbers, fishermen and shirt makers.
has been definitely established.
And the principal place of those CONTACTS is through the
office of Boyd Bros., in Cristobal, agents for the Japanese stean-
ship line, OSAKA SHOSEN KAISHA.

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
In full justice to my countryman, ROBERT J. BOYD, head
of that firm, let me hasten to say that there is not the slightest
suspicion that he personally has even a remote idea of what has
been going on, or that he has any knowledge whatever of the
frequent clandestine meetings between one of his German em-
ployees and Mr. T. TAHARA, or that he even dreams of the
wellworn trail that connects his offices in Cristobal with one of
the leading Japanese barbershops and shirt factories in Colon, or
that he ever saw or was ever told of the information that has
passed direct from Japanese places of business in Colon,
THROUGH THE HANDS OF THIS GERMAN EMPLOYEE
into the possession of Mr. T. TAHARA and into his secret files
at Hotel Washington for transmission to Japan, via Vice Admiral
Hykutake.
If I am CRAZY, I dare you to have me committed to Corozal.
If these be LIES, I dare you to hale me into court and I chal-
lenge you to ask for PROOFS UNDER OATH AND BY COM-
PETENT EVIDENCE in any court of competent jurisdiction.
I have a personal interest in the PANAMA CANAL. My old
father and mother toiled for more than fifty years and PAID
TAXES to help PAY FOR IT. I myself sweat and swore for
weeks amid sand flies and rain on Margarita Island to help build
the fortifications, at what is now Fort Randolph, intended t
DEFEND IT. And it is my growing grandsons whose blood may
someday be shed in its DEFENSE — in defense of the KEY-
STONE of safety of American institutions.
It is only pussy-footers, cowards and the blind who now re-
fuse to admit that Japan and the United States are at the brink
of WAR — a war which will be avoided by all possible honorablo
means on the part of the United States, and postponed until the
very last day in the hopes of averting it — but a war which, to
all well informed now appears to be inevitable, eventually.
And when I see Japanese like T. TAHARA openly, brazenly
and defiantly carrying on operations which threaten the very
foundations of our national safety and jeopardize our national
defense and imperil the lives of our sons and grandsons, it makes
my BLOOD BOIL and I SEE RED, and I believe that only by
LETTING THE PEOPLE KNOW THE TRUTH will the country
be SAFE.

=== Page 139 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosre No. /. to Despatch No. /533. of June . 1933.
Clipping from THE PANAMA AMERICAN of May 19, 1933. PANAMA, R. deP.
AMERICAN LEGATION
Rambling, Gambling and Publishing
By NELSON ROUNSEVELL
(From "The Washington Daily News", Scripps-Howard newspaper)
I was most ardently URGED by a friend with high-up Army
connections to attend the Pacific Sector Review at Ft. Amador
yesterday, and I was strongly impelled to accept the generous
and, I believe, sincere invitation. But other reasons outweighed
those inclinations, and I did not go, much as I would have liked
to see the Isthmus' finest military wares on display, and proud
as I know I would have been of the show put on for the special
benefit and edification of the visiting Japanese.
In the first place, 8 o'clock in the morning is mighty early
for an old night-hawk like me to make an appearance. And it is
the time too when I am confronted seven times a week with a
day's work to do — a day crammed full of more responsibilities,
obligations and hard work than is ever possible to accomplish.
I have not witnessed an Army Review since the last one
that I participated in in Coblenz, Germany, when in 1919 Genera!
Pershing inspected our sector of the Army of Occupation. I don't
know what he may have thought of our appearance, but I know
that I counted him as being the finest appearing man I had ever
seen. The sight of Pershing was an inspiration to me and made
an impression that I have never forgotten. Never have I seen a
uniform worn so completely perfect as his; never have I observed
demeanor so impressive; nor do I ever expect to see a bearing
so intensely MILITARY in every word and gesture as was his
that day as he alighted from his car and stood at attention while
we passed before him in review.
The other high-light of that Review was some hours before
the General's arrival when we were inspected by our own officers
and some minor visiting generals and colonels. We stood at
rigid attention for what seemed like an interminable time with
our equipment spread in orderly array on a blanket in front of
us while the file of officers marched deliberately past with all
eyes focused on our personal. appearance and equipment — and
woe to him who was discovered with a missing button, an un-
shined shoe, a cap askew or a missing tooth brush.
When the time came to visually inventory my equipment,
the sharp eyes of a colonel registered the absence of a COMB.
Said he, "WHERE IS YOUR COMB?"
I raised my hand as if to salute, took my cap off gracefully,
exposing my bald head to the colonel's gaze and said, "SIR, I
HAVE NO USE FOR ONE."
Without cracking a smile and without sacrificing even a
small fraction of his erect, military bearing and dignity, the Col-
lonel removed his own cap a few inches, exposing an equally
shiney pate, and said, "I UNDERSTAND," and passed on with-
out further comment.
As a matter of strict fact, I have never seen a Military Re-
view except the few that I participated in, and if I never seo
another it will be plenty soon enough. I have no particular han-
kering for reminders, be they ever so impressive, of the WAR
scenes that I participated in, and I could not enjoy the thoughts
which would be uppermost in my mind — of speculating on just
how soon those finely clad, expertly drilled boys who passed in
review would be drenched in blood, their uniforms caked with
mud and their hearts filled with the dread that in 1918 I read in
so many of my comrade's faces and felt in my own heart — the
dread and uncertainty of whether it would be the next shell —
or the next — or the next — that would spell the END — and
of whether that end would be one of shattered limbs and mangled
guts or whether in God's Mercy it would be over quickly.
Armies are, I suppose, like hospitals, necessary accoutre-
ments of modern civilization (7) but I am unable to conjure up
pleasant or happy thoughts in connection with either. And the
fewer reminders I have of both - and the sordid pain and suf-

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
tering that goes with both — the greater my peace of mind. Hav-
ing miraculously escaped unscathed from some rather harrowing
experiences in both, hospitals and the army, I am content to
wend my way as a peaceful (?) civilian and leave it to those of
a younger, less experienced generation to applaud and go into
patriotic ecstacy over the impressive appearance of young man-
hood clad in the raiment of and laden down with the impedi-
menta of our FIGHTING FORCES.
None-the-less, I would not do away with them, nor handicap
them, nor belittle or embarrass them (except it be a General
now and then) nor oppose their forward stride in Military Per-
fection and Efficiency — for, until the ways of man and God (as
those ways today are taught) are changed, Heaven knows we
MUST have armies, just as we MUST have hospitals — and I
applaud with YOU the scientific strides made by the one in art
of TAKING LIFE and by the other in that of SAVING LIFE.
Thus strange and contradictory and inconsistent are the
ways of MAN and thus blindly do we tread the misnamed paths
of PROGRESS (?). But I want you to know that my approval
and applause of OUR ARMY is based on the same impulses
which at remote periods of my life caused me to carry a six-
shooter within easy reach — not because of any desire on my
part to KILL, but because I deemed it as perhaps the only pos-
sible means of saving my own life. And so I view our ARMY
— with horror at the thought of the needless KILLING it may
some day be called upon to do, but with the conviction that, un-
der modern standards of politics and religion, it is the only po
sible means of preserving our national existence.
Another reason for refraining from attendance at yesterday's
Military Review is that HYPOCRISY irks my soul. I hate sham
and pretense. I respect only plainly spoken truths, be they of
friendship or animosity, hurled straight into the faces of those
to whom and of whom we speak.
And no sample of HYPOCRISY that I have ever witnessed
smells more strongly to High Heaven, or reeks more of national
insincerity than that which has been on display this week be-
tween the visiting Japanese, on their so-called mission of amity
and good-will, and the welcoming hosts of officialdom, American
and Panamanian.
I might have admired Japanese Vice Admiral Hyakutake and
even Chief Japanese Secret Service Man T. Tahara (a veteran
steamship man has told me that Tahara knows absolutely no-
thing of the SHIPPING business) had they had the guts and
been imbued with sufficient honesty and sincerity to come out
boldly and say:
"How do you do, YANKS? We Japs have just come to look
you over and make our plans to work on YOU just as soon as we
get through cleaning up on the Chinks. And what the hell are
you going to do about it?"
And I would have applauded, had Uncle Sam have sent
some high ranker down to the dock who had the guts to stick
out his hand and say:
"Fair enough, Japs. Come on and look us over, but damn
well watch yuor step while you are here, and don't for one minute
think you are kidding us a bit. And, by the way, when you leave
you might as well take this bird Tahara and about 90 percent of
your barbers, shirt makers and fishermen back with you — for,
if you don't, we are mighty soon going to set them afloat — we've
stood for about all of their funny business with cameras and
sounding instruments that we are going to."
Perhaps it has been a MISTAKE for me to speak thus
plainly into the faces of our Japanese visitors — but it is my
way and I can't help it — and, besides, I wanted the satisfaction
of SAYING what thousands of others on the Isthmus (including
some high-rankers) were THINKING — but, because of offi-
cial handicaps and diplomatic precedents, and holding their
johs, and maintaining their dignity, and what not thay COULD
NOT SAY.

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. /. to Despatch No. /533. of June 2/. 1933.
PANxMA. 10P
JAPS GETTING BOLDER
Boyd Bros.' Steamship Agency office in Cris-
tobal, Canal Zone, continues to be the surreptitious
meeting place between JAPANESE SECRET
SERVICE MAN — T. TAHARA — and the GER-
MAN CLERK who works there.
Both were together in conference UNTIL AF-
TER MIDNIGHT Friday night in the steamship of-
fice.
It may be all right — Bob Boyd apparently must
think it is  but that is rather an unusual hour to
be transacting STEAMSHIP BUSINESS — especial-
ly for a line that handled a total of LESS THAN 50
TONS OrCARGO for Cristobal in one month this
year.
And, besides, Mr. Tahara knows next to nothing
about the SHIPPING business. Quite recently wlten
approached by the representative of a European
steamship line with a view to trans-shipping cargo
for the Orient via the Osaka Shosen Kaisha, the
line which, according to his visiting card is rep-
resented by Mr. Tahara, the latter said he was NOT
INTERESTED. Then what in hell does he expect us
to believe that he IS INTERESTED IN?
MORE — last Thursday, on the eve of sailing,
a Japanese Naval man in full uniform, from either
the "Iwate" or the "Yakumo", visited 12 different
JAPANESE BARBERSHOPS IN PANAMA CITY
carrying with him a leather brief case from which
he delivered to the BARBERS (?) documents bear-
ing the official JAPANESE GOVERNMENT SEAL.
There is something for you doubting Thomases
to chew on for a few days, and something for Amer-
ican and Panamanian OFFICIALDOM to ponder
well.
NELSON ROUNSEVELL.

AMEY SANLERATION
Clipping from THE PANAMA AMERICaN of May 22, 1933.
Rambling, Gambling and Publishing
By Nelson Rounsevell
The following letter, obviously from a Britisher, and th
article which accompanied it are reproduced merely as interest
ing discussion of a very important topic, although not entirel
in accord with my own views.
En route to New York. U. S. A.
May 18th. 1933.
Mr. Nelson Rounsevell.
The Panama American,
Dear Sir.
En route to New York I read the cditorial in which you
pointed out the treatment meted out to the Peruvian cruisers
when they docked at Balbou and that afford'd to the Japanese
cruisers on their arrival here. The reason for the difference in
courtesies is obvious.
I also read your editcrial of May 17th. and I qucstion wheth-
er any other paper in the world has ured to state such facts.
Quite right — only pussy-footers, cowards and the blind can
now refuse to admit that Japun and the United States are on the
brink of war.
Whether or not you wil see fit to publish the following ar
ticle I am writing is a matter for yourself. But you are vers
strong; if you do I am hoping it may give many in Greut Britain
as well as the United States, furiously to think.
Cordially yours.
A. ZEUS
THE THREAT OF THE EAST
By A. ZEUS.
The underlying meaning and inner history of the saturnalic
of welcome afforded the Admiral. officers and cadets of the
Japanese warships now in Panama. and that which was recently
given to them in the United States, are quite obvious to every
thinking person. The fulsome praise and eulogies that streamed
forth from the mouths of highly placed personages and officials
in America and Panama, are desperate efforts on the part of the
United States to curry favor with Japan and are symptomatic o
one fact; the Government of the United States, knowing the
menace which confronts it. is afraid. The Disarmament plea tc
the world of Mr. Ramsay MacDonald and President Roosevel
at this critical moment is a frantic attempt to avert that which
draws steadily closer — WAR.
For years the public and governments of hoth the United
States and Great Britain have been warned of this eastern peril.
by eminent writers in England and by the Hearst Press in Ame-
rica; but the warnings have fallen on deaf ears. The difference
between Japan and those two countries is this; Japan has had
no illusions; vision, spirit and a great ideal have been her moti-
vating power. The people of the United States and Great Britain
have been saturating themselves with luxury, have devoted
their entire thoughts and energies to the worship of the Goldem
Calf. Great Britain has been split into various political factions.
each reviling the other, each seeking to gain power until today
there is no longer any solid unity. The people of the United
States have been bled white by the Wall Street octopus and the
Banks, and Cangress has spent futile years, months and weeks
wrangling and fighting ober the Eighteenth Amendment and thres-
point-two per cent beer. Meanwhile the Japanese, coldly and
calculatingly, have formulated their plans with the definite ideal
of creating an Empire second to none.
China was their first objective. Ceaseless warfare is now
taking place in Manchukuo; day after day the Japanese are ad-
vancing, consolidating their position and milttarizing the people.
But this is only part of a much greater and more ambitioue plans
Their punitive expedition against Shanghai had one main objock
It was ludicrous, as the Japanese well know, to attempt to ine

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
vade China and take Shanghai with fifty thousand men, This
expedition was merely strategic, its object being to discover
whether any one or a combination of the Great Powers' would
definitely command them to stop. We know the result; the
Great Powers were afraid. Not a single ultimatum was issued.
Subsequently the League of Nations put up a brave front. JAPAN
WARNED THE LEAGUE THAT IF THEIR VIEWS DID NOT
CONFORM TO HER OWN SHE WOULD WALK OUT. Bu*
greatly daring and with a united front the other countries com-
prising the League of Nations did not agree to Japan's terms.
and the Nipponese Government, defying the entire world with
a contemptuous gesture, kept its word. On this momentous day
the writing was on the wall for every thinking government and
the whole world to read. NO NATION COULD HAVE DEFIED
THE REST OF THE WORLD UNLESS THERE WAS SOME
GREAT AND POWERFUL REASON UNDERLING ITS AC-
TION.
The secret of Jupan's strength and justified arrogance today
is that she has evolved three en gines of destruction so terrible in
their forer and so typically Oriental as to be incone vuble to the
Occidenta' mind. The first is a torpedo that carries a human be-
ing inside who steers it unerringly to its mark. Nuturally tho
man within is blown to utoms. The second is her one-man air
plane, virtually a flying corpedo, which also can unerringly strike
its objective, be it tuttle hip. fortific un cr arsenal, and again
the human director is blown to pieces. But with Oriental fanati-
cism the honor of these positions is coveted and clamored for.
for the men who go to their deaths in this way attain to aternal
ylory.
THERE IS A THIRD ENGINE OF DESTRUCTION — A
NEW TYPE OF GAS. IT HAS BEEN TRIED EXPERIMENT-
ALLY IN MANCHUKUO. LEAVING BEHIND MATTER
WHICH A FEW MOMENTS BEFORE WAS MEN.
Unquestionably these facts are known to the governments
of the United States and Great Britain. It is quite obhvious that
in Panama. guns. hattleships. airplanes and land forces are im-
potent to avert the destruction of the Canal, the power plant and
the pumping station as and when the Japanese choose to bring
it about. There are plenty of faithful sons of Japan on the isthmus
who will. when they receive THE WORD. carry this out.
At the moment on the islands stringing across the Pacific
are colonies of Japanese. The entire business section of Hono-
lulu, for example, is now controlled by these people, and they are
seeking fresh bases in South and Central America; slowly they
are pushing towards their ultimate objective — the United States
of America.
But equally serious is the position of Great Britain. At im-
mense cost her government has built the great Singapore base;
but of what use is this against the one-man airplane which can-
not miss its mark? Then there is Australia, that vast country
so sparsely populated and utterly defenseless; Japan knows the
half a million men landed in Australia could seize and hold 
continent and could not possibly be driven out owing, yet again, 
her oneman airplane and other terrible engines of war. The British
fleet would be important. Japan would be master of the seas
in that regfon, and could rush all the supplies and reinforcements
that she chose without let or hindrance.
There is only one hope of averting world disaster — yes,
even at the eleventh hour it is just possible; and alliance between
the English-speaking peoples. United and shoulder to shoulder,
the United States and Great Britain may yet be powerful enough
to withstand the ambitions of this Oriental Napoleonism. Dis-
armament will only strengthen Japan and weakën other nations,
because Japan cmnnot possibly be a signatory and in any case
wouldnot.
THE FATE OF THE WORLD HANGS IN THE BALANCE.
THE POSITION OF THE UNITED STATES AND GREAT
BRITAIN IS CRITICAL: THEY ARE FACING DISASTER
AND THEY BOTH KNOW FULL WELL THAT SINGLY
WAR WITH JAPAN WOULD MEAN DEFEAG

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.96)
219.
FAR EASTERN AFFA
JAN 20 1934
TAPAN.
December 20, 1955.
Department of State
Cruise of the Japanese Training Squadron.
(Extracts from address by Captain M. Niimi, Commanding Officer,
H.I.J.M.S. Yakumo).
An address describing the cruise of the Japanese Training
Squadron to the United States during the spring of 193s was
delivered at the Naval Reserve Officers Club in Tokyo. The follow-
ing extracts are sent as being of possible interest to the
Depertment.
The total number of midshipmen making the cruise was
126 line, 34 engineering, 14 paymaster midshipmen, and in addition
8
32 special medical officers.
6
The cruise was begun, just ut the height of the discussion
in Geneva when the international situation was very delicate. For
3
this reason every possible precaution was taken and preparations
31
to meet every emergeney were made. The plan was to have the
Squadron at the highest possible state of efficiency.
Target practices were held twice - once off Chinkai and
one off Tateyama.
CJ
While in the United tates all hands were kept so busy
that there was little time to study conditions or make obser-
vations.
The cordial reception at Port Townsend, the first port
of call, made a good impression on the whole S uadron, and was in
considerable contrast to the misgivings about their possible
reception in the United States which had bee feit when the
Squadron left Japan.
The reception at all ports was so cordial - especially
that by the local military and naval authorities - that it
appeared that there must have been special instructions sent out
from Washington.
The Commandant at Bremerton was extremely friendly and
kind to the officers, had them to lunch and showed them the
entire Navy Yard. The midshipmen were also shown through the
yard, and Astoria (being built) as well as New York and
Tennessee being repaired.
The reception of the Squadron at San Franoisco was most
oordial. During the stay the ships went alongside the pier
instead of anchoring as usual - which was considered by the
Japanese residants to be of special significance. The midshipmen
wore showm through Mare Island and part of the officers were
present at the dedication of Sunnyvale Air Station. The effort
of the Navy to interest the publio in maritime affairs through
the showing of Constitution was very interesting to all the
2
Japanese officers.
16
3
The opportunity to exchange courtesies with the officors
of the United States Fleet at San Pedro was most welcome - and
the efforts made by all of the American officers from the
Commander in Chier down were greatly appreciated. The midshipmen
vimited Saratoga, Nevada and West Virginia, and there were
mamerous parties given aboard the American ships. These ware
F. Hearst's "Examiner" was friendly.

=== Page 150 === (confidence 0.98)
2
The cordial reception by the Commandant at Balboa, by the
Co mander Special Service Squadron and the Command ing General was
imilar to hat with which they had received elsewhere. Both
offieers end midshipmen were allowed to inspeot the Air Station
and Submarine Base at Coco solo. Wo were all impressed by the
Panama Canal - but could not help feeling sorry for Panama, a
country which has no armaments and must depend on others to defend
them.
The stay of the uadron in lonolulu was made very pleasant
by the local authorities, the Nuvy, the rmy and the oople. The
midshipmen were shown earl turhor nd a revlew of the troops near
Diamond Head was hold.
There is no oubt that our visit helped much in furthering
our relations with the United tates. e cannot account for the
extreme cordielity of our reception, but we are lad that we were
so well received.
Japanese in the United tates have had much diffioulty in
bringing up their children in the Japanese way. Most of the second
generation falt that Japan was a small unimportent country.
However, as a result of our defying the world over Manchukuo and
china their ideas have changed. The number of those studying
Japanese has inoreased remerkably in the last two years. The
question of whether these people would remain loyal to the United
States in a Japanese-American war is a diffioult one. At any rate
most of them are proud of their Japanese.ancestry.
Japanese in Canada - mostly fishermen - seem to be having
a dif ioult time. Anti-Japnese feeling is very strong. and they
are disorimineted acainst.
Mexico is in reality quite friendly toward Japan, but the
influence of the United states is predominant. As a resuit the
Mexicans desire above all to remain on good terms with the United
States and hesitate to show their feelings too freely.
On this cruise, instead of byying coal in the ports we
visited, two steamers from Japan were sent with Japanese coal. We
coaled seven times from these steemers.
Wherever we went we were greeted like brothers by the
Japanese residents, who al so did everything possible to entertain
us. Also they were unxious to hear our officers address them.
While we were in America, 35 speeches to Japanese audiences were
made by officers.
On our way back we visited Jaluit, Beown, Truk, Saipon,
Wotje, Palao, eto., and were all impressed by the great importance
of these 1slands in our scheme of national defense.
The cruise was a most successful one. Our officers and men
learned muah and the results of the training were excellent. Our
opportunity to see the whole American fleet at Los Angeles was
one of a life time.
Note:- The Naval Attache notes that there is a considerable
differenee in our poliey in showing important naval
yards and stations to forelgn visitors and the Japanese
poliey of not showing anything to foreign officers even
on inspections which are supposedly on an exchange basis.

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.99)
Batavia-C., December 27, 1934.
reatment of Japanese naval tankers
in Californian ports.
I have the honor to request that you supply me, if pos-
sible, with information respecting the following problem.
As you may probably know the Imperial Japanese Navy has at
its disposal a number of tankers, which are continually add-
ing to the fuel oil stocks of the said vavy and for which
purpose they call regularly at the oil ports of Borneo and
California.
The Japanese navy has given these ships, which have
the appearance of merchant vessels, the status of warships
Under this interpretation these ships would enjoy when
visiting foreign ports the privileges generally accorded to
warships, such as exemption from pilot and harbor dues.
On the other hand they would then not be at liberty
to enter foreign commercial ports without previous noti-
fication, which merchant vessels are at liberty to do but
warships in general not.
I should be pleased to learn from you what treatment
is given to these naval tankers in the Californian ports,
whether they are exempted from pilot and harbor dues, etc.,
and whether each visit to the Californian ports is an-
nounced previously by the Japanese government as is usual
for warships in general.
The names of the Japanese tankers are: Erimo,
Hayatomo, Iro, Naruto, Ondo, Sata, Shiretoko, Shiriya,
Tsurumi.
I avail myself of this opportunity to renew to you,
Sir, the assurance of my highest consideration.
The Chief of Naval Staff in
Netherland India,
(Signed) Coster.
To the
Consul General of the
United States of
America,
Batavia.

WASHINGTON
DIVISION OF FOREIGN
SERVICE ADMINISTRATION
February 7, 1935.
The Honorable,
FEB 1 1 35
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
The Secretary of State.
Sir:
I beg leave to acknowledge the receipt of your third person
communication, dated January 29, 1935, File Number FA 894. 3311/184,
transmitting therewith a copy of a dispatch from the American Consulate
894.3
General at Batavia, Java, requesting information as to whether in United
States ports unarmed Japanese tankers are required to undergo quarantine
inspection, or any other necessary quarantine treatment, and whether
fees are charged for such services.
0.
The United States does not extend special privileges as re-
gards quarantine formalities to unarmed naval fuel or supply ships of
any nation. Such vessels are treated on the same basis as commercial
vessels and are sub ject to the same charges for any quarantine services
rendered. While it is practice of the United States, in the interest
86
of international comity, to exercise purely nominal quarantine super-
vision over armed vessels of friendly powers paying visits to United
States ports in the nature of intern tional courtesy, for which no charge
is therefore necessary, nevertheless in the case of unarmed vessels,
owned and operated by the naval or other official establishments of for-
eign nations which come to the United States for commercial reasons,
such vessels are required, under the qu rantine laws of the United States,
to undergo quarantine inspection and any other necessary quarantine treat-
ment upon arrival at a United States port, for which quarantine services
the assessment of charges is made.
In this connection, it may be of interest to also refer to Trea-
sury Department letters addressed to the State Department under date of
Apri1 10, 1920 and December 20, 1928, File Number FA 158. 001/2162.
By direction of the Secretary.
FE8 8 6 1935
Respectfully,
FILED
F/FG
Jonerchine Roche
ssistant Secretary of the Treasury.

RVICE ADMINISTRATION
Sir:
Acknowledgment is made of State Department note of 29 January,
file FA - 894.3311/184, together with its inelosure, copy of a letter
dated 28 December, 1934, from the American Consul at Batavia inclosing
a copy of a letter dated 27 December from the Chief of Naval Staff in
894.331
Netherland India, requesting information as to the privileges, if any,
accorded Japanese unarmed naval tankers when visiting ports in the
United States. In particular, information is requested from this
department as to whethor it is necessary thet each visit of an un-
armed Japanese naval tanker to a United States port be made the
subject of prior notification, as is usual for warships in general.
I am pleased to inform you that, except for a reciprocal agree-
ment between our own Government and the Japanese Government with respect
to visits of unarmed naval ships to United States ports in the Philippine
87
Islands and to ports in Japan, there is no authority for visits of unarmed
Japanese naval tan:ers to United States ports without prior notification
through diplom.tic channels.
On 7 April, 1923, this department was inromed by the Japanese
Naval Attache of the proposed visits of five Japanese naval tankers to
San Pedro, California. On 10 Arril, 1923, the Naval Attache was informed
by this department that every facility tould be offered by naval forces on
the West Coast and further that it was assumed that the Ja anese Embassy
had sent the usual notification to the Depurtment of State. On 13 April,
1923, the Japanose Naval Attache, in reply, stated that his Embassy would
inform the State De urtment with respect to the visits of the Japanese
tankers.
From 1923 until 1932, this dep rtment received information concern-
ing proposed visits of Japanese tankers to West Coast ports from the Japanese
Naval Attache. Since 1932 no such notifications have been received although
a number of visits to Ulited States ports have been made by Japanese naval
tankers.
It is assumed that information reguested by the Chief of Naval
Staff in Netherland India, : ith respect to pilot and h rbor dues,
obtained from the federul der rtment concerned.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
The Department has received yourletter of the 29th ultimo
STRTION
your reference FA-894.3311/184, enclosing a copy of a dispatch
with enclosure from the American Consul General at Batavia. Java,
concerning the desire of the authorities of Netherland India to
894.3311
DIVIVIN FO
obtain information as to the privileges if any, accorded Japanese
3
unarmed naval tankers when visiting ports of the United States.
In particular, information is requested as to whether such vessels
CO
are subject to pilotage and harbor dues.
8.5.8
BERVI
This Department has consistently held that Japanese naval
tankers are not required to enter, clear, or pay navigation fees
or tonnage taxes, arriving in our ports only for bunker oil or
coal.
8
Should such tankers be engaged in the transportation of car-
8
goes in the course of commerce, they would be treated in our ports
as if they were vessels mgaged in commerce, and entry and clearance
and other federal fees would be collected.
As you are aware, charges for pilotage are imposed by state
pilots organizations and this Department is not prepared to answer
the question in regard to such charges or in rerard to harbor dues
other than those imposed under the federal laws.
Very truly yours,
2
3
Assistant Secretary of Commerce

CONVERSATION:
235
9 02
Mr. Shun-ichiro Kawahara,
Third Secretary, Japanese Embassy.
107
Mr. Dooman.
Subject:
Entry into American Ports of Japanese
Public Vessels.
894.3
Mr. Kawahara referred to a recent note from the
894.3311
Department to the Japanese Embassy, drawing attention to
3
the fact that Japanese naval tankers, some of them armed,
had formed the practice of entering American ports without
notification through diplomatic channels, and requesting
C0
6
that previous notification of such visits be made through
official channels. He said that, according to the records
of the Embassy, when the informal arrangement of March,
1931, with regard to the entry on local notice of American
and Japanese unarmed public vessels to Japanese and
Philippine ports, respectively, was under consideration,
MAY!
FILED
the Japanese Embassy had proposed that the arrangement
cover the entry of Japanese unarmed public vessels to
ports in the United States, but that the late Mr. Miller
had stated that reference to ports in the United States
would be unnecessary, for the reason that no question would
arise as to the propriety of Japanese vessels entering on
/FG.
local notice.
I

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
I said to Mr. Kawahara that, according to a memorandum
prepared by Mr. Miller, dated June 3, 1931, Mr. Maki, of
the Japanese Embassy, had presented a draft memorandum
outlining the procedure to be followed in cases covered
by the arrangement, and that that draft had been unacceptable
to us for the reason that "it included visits of Japanese
vessels to ports in continental United States as well as
to the Philippine Islands, whereas the discussions held,
and the understanding reached, related only to visits to
the Philippine Islands". I pointed out to Mr. Kawahara
that it seemed to be clear that the arrangement is not
applicable to Japanese tankers, whether armed or unarmed,
entering ports in the United States.
Mr. Kawahara then stated that these tankers had been
entering American ports on local notice since 1918 or 1919,
and he inquired in regard to the reason for a change in the
practice.
to he laboring
I replied that Mr. Kawahara appeared under some
misapprehension: according to a letter from our Navy
Department, previous notice of the arrival of Japanese
tankers had been given by the Japanese Naval Attaché to the
Navy Department until 1932, since which time these vessels
had been entering without any notice whatever. The entry
of

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
of armed tankers had drawn the attention of the authorities
to the departure from the established practice, but in
addition there had been noted instances of Japanese
residents of Los Angeles returning from Japanese tankers
with sake which they had attempted to pass surreptitiously
through the customs, and one or two other instances which
were of a disquieting character.
Mr. Kawahara asked whether we would agree to an
extension of the arrangement of 193l so as to cover visits
to United States ports. I replied that that was a matter
with regard to which the views of the Navy Department would
be of particular importance but that if the Japanese
Embassy desired to make such proposal the Department would
be prepared to refer it to the appropriate authorities.
m.mH

appropriate channels. Specific instances that have been
noted are the visits of H.I.J.M.S. TSURUMI to Honolulu
on December 2, 1934, and to San Pedro on December 15,
1934, and of H.I.J.M.S. NARUTo to San Pedro on December 26,
1934, and to Honolulu on January 12, 1935. It 1s under-
stood that each of the vessels above-mentioned carried
two 3-ineh anti-aircraft guns.

the Japanese Ambassador, the Secretary of State requests
that His Exoelleney will be so good as to cause steps to
be taken in order that there may be conformity by publie

CONVERSATION:

Subject:

Mr. Kawahara referred to the conversation which we
189
CN
had on March 9/and stated he had conferred with the Japanese
Ambassador and with the Japanese Naval Attaché. The Naval
Attaché had not been aware that his predecessors had, until
6
O
1932, followed the practice of giving previous notification
to the Navy Department of the visits of Japanese naval
tankers, but he had consulted his records and found that
such was indeed the case.
Mr. Kawahara then handed me the attached paper in
which there are put three questions. I replied that I
would refer the matter informally to the Navy Department
and to other departments and give him a reply in due course.
Mr. Kawahara said that his inquiry was entirely informal,
MAY !
FILED
for the reason that the Embassy is not acting under
Japauese
instruction from the Navy Department. He said that in
fact he had no information whatever in regard to the attitud
which his Government might take concerning the suggestion
that

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
that the practice be revived of giving notification of

arrangement of 193l be extended to cover ports in the
United States.
NOTE:
Commander Lewis, Navy Operations, Navy
Department called on me this morning after my conversation
with Mr. Kawahara, as above-reported. I furnished
Commander Lewis with a copy of the paper handed me by
Mr. Kawahara and requested him to let me know informally
what the attitude of the Navy Department would be in the
event that the Japanese Embassy definitely made a proposal
along the lines of questions 2 or 3 therein. Commander
Lewis said that he would discuss the matter with his
associates in the Navy Department and that he would reply
to me in due course.

MEMORANDUM
Commander Zacharias, C.N.I., Navy Department,
called on me this morning in regard to the three
894.3311/170
questions put to me on March 11, 1935, by Mr. Kawahara
894.3311/1
of the Japanese Embassy regarding visits of unarmed naval

190
Commander Zacharias stated that:
894.3311
1. The only American unarmed naval vessel to

90
1931 entered Japanese ports on local notification;
2. The Navy Department would not agree to
the entry of Japanese vessels into United States ports
solely on notification to the Navy Department by the
Japanese Naval Attaché; and
3. The Navy Department is satisfied with the
FILED

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.98)
February 18 1935.
894.3311
no recond
Dear Mr. van der Wyek:
With reference to your inquiry of February 14
concerning the treatment of Japanese naval tankers in
894.3311/191A
United States ports, I find that it has been for several
years the oustom of the Japanese Embassy to notify the
Department of State of the proposed visits of Japanese
naval tankers and that there is no authority for visits
of such tankers to United States ports without prior
notification through diplomatic channels except for a
reciprocal agreement with respect to visits of unarmed
naval ships to United States ports in the Philippine
Islands and to ports in Japan. Recently two Japanese
naval tankers visited United States ports without prior
notifioation but it is the intention of the Department to
bring to the attention of the Japanese Government the
necessity of such notification in the future.
Unarmed
Jonkheer H. M. van der Wyok,
Seoretary,
Netherland Legation.

=== Page 180 === (confidence 0.93)
-2-
Unarmed Japanese naval tankers are not required to
enter, clear, or pay navigation fees or tonnage taxes,
but they are subject to the same charges as commeroial
vessels for any quarantine services rendered. As pilotage
charges are not levied by the Federal authorities but by
State pilot's organizations, I am not in a position to
say whether or not Japanese naval tankers are required to
pay for the services of pilots.
Sincerely yours,
Pierroront Hopfat

894.3311

6
2
***********
Mr. Kawahara then stated that in view of the reply
made to him by me he would like to leave with me an
informal letter addressed to me by Mr. Miura, First

to Los Angeles and Honolulu of the Japanese naval tanker

0.

insistence that visits of such vessels should be notified

distinctly understood by Mr. Miura that communicating
in such manner information in regard to visits of
Japanese naval vessels would not in the future be
regarded as compliance with the prescribed practice.

Mr. Shun-ichiro Kawahara,
FAR EASTENN AP
Third Secretary, Japanese Embassy.
1A
Mr. Dooman.
894.3311
Subject:
Entry into American Ports of Japanese Unarmed
Public Vessels.
Mr. Kawahara began by requesting that I bear in mind
894.
that what he would have to say to me would be entirely
informal, for the reason that he had not discussed the
31
matter with the Ambassador. He laid before me two alternative
drafts of a note which he would like to have the Department
send the Japanese Embassy in regard to the visits of
6
Japanese unarmed naval vessels to United States ports, and
9
he asked whether I would study them and inform him whether
or not it would be possible to effect an arrangement which
would enable such Japanese vessels to enter our ports
MAY 1
without notification through diplomatic channels.
FILED
One draft contemplated that the Department would
inform the Japanese Embassy that this Government would be
agreeable to reversion by the Japanese to their former
practice of making notification through the consul within
whose district the ports lie which the vessels propose to
F/FG
enter,

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
enter, or by the Japanese Naval Attaché direct to the
Navy Department. I told Mr. Kawahara that I would have
nothing to do with such a proposal, for the reason that
the practices to which it was proposed to revert were
entirely irregular. Furthermore, I reminded Mr. Kawahara
that I had informed him only a few days previously that
the Navy Department would not agree to any such arrangement.
The second draft is attached. I pointed out to Mr.
Kawahara that it had been formulated in such manner as to
convey a thoroughly misleading impression of the actual
circumstances. I reminded him that it was the Japanese
Government which had originally proposed the arrangement,
effected in l931, covering visits of unarmed public vessels
to Japanese and Philippine ports, respectively, and that the
records indicate very clearly that the Japanese Government
had no misunderstanding of the scope of the arrangement.
Mr. Kawahara expressed assent. I further pointed out that,
whereas only one visit every three months was made by an
American unarmed public vessel to Japanese ports, namely, the
GOLD STAR which visits the port of Miike every quarter, some
fifteen to twenty Japanese tankers visit American and Philip-
pine ports each year. I expressed the opinion that, therefore,
the condition of reciprocity would be, from our point of
view, almost entirely theoretical. I said that it would
not be very inconvenient for us to make arrangements through
diplomatic

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
diplomatic channels for the infrequent visits of the
GOLD STAR to Japanese ports, and that I felt almost certain
1n-
that we would prefer that the formal arrangement of 1931
be brought to an end rather than to agree to any such
proposal as that envisaged in Mr. Kawahara's draft.
Mr. Kawahara appeared to be somewhat disappointed in
the character of my reply. He said that the Japanese Navy
Department could not understand why this Government had
suddenly and without warning terminated the practice by
which Japanese vessels entered our ports on local notifica-
tion or on notification by the Japanese Naval Attaché to
the Navy Department. I said that I had no way of knowing
whether the Navy Department would have continued indefinitely
to acquiesce in any such irregular practice; but that it was
to be assumed that our Government could not acquiesce in
armed Japanese vessels coming into our ports without formal
notification.
After further conversation, during which the ground
already gone over was again covered, I said to Mr. Kawahara
that I could see no hope of any arrangement being made
"on condition of reciprocity", but if, however, the Japanese
Government was prepared to request that this Government
permit, purely as an act of comity and for purposes of
convenience, unarmed Japanese naval vessels to enter United
States ports, I would sound out opinion in this Department
and

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
and in other interested departments. Mr. Kawahara asked
that I let him know in due course whether some courtesy
along the above line could be extended.
COMMENT:
Whether this Government could or would agree to the
entry into United States ports of Japanese unarmed public
vessels on local notification or on notification by the
Japanese Naval Attaché to the Navy Department, would involve
consideration among other matters of whether such notifica-
tion would be apt to create inconveniences in regard to
availability of anchorages and docking facilities, movements
of American naval vessels, and so on.
It would be necessary to give consideration also to
whether it would be necessary or desirable to extend to
vessels of other natlons the privilege which would be
extended to Japanese unarmed public vessels, and to the
question whether it would be desirable to depart from the
universal practice of requiring notification through
diplomatic channels.
It is to be particularly noted that the privilege would
be extended ex gratia; that it would be a unilateral act on
our part and not conditioned on reciprocity (for the reason
that the entry on local notification of American vessels
into Japanese ports is provided for in the arrangement of
1931); and that this Government, having assumed no obligation,
coula

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.97)
- 5 -
could withdraw the privilege at any time it saw fit.
On the other hand, it would be reasonable to suppose that,
if the Japanese were not scrupulous in their exercise of
this privilege and it became necessary to withdraw the
privilege, there would be more ill-feeling engendered
than if this Government were to continue to insist upon
the observance by the Japanese of the prescribed and usual
procedure.

APR 9- 1935
April 6,1935
The Department of State has been happy
to give favorable consideration to the desire of
the Japanese Government, expressea by Mr. Kawahara
in conversation with Mr. Dooman on April
1935, that the American Government be willing
to accord reciprocally to Japanese transports
and other unarmed public vessels visiting ports
in the United States si ilar treatment to that
which has been given by the Japanese Government
to Ameriaan transports and other unarmed public
vessels visiting ports in Japan.
In view of this the Japanese Consul
is to notify the local authorities in the
United States at least two weeks in advance,
the name of the vessel and of the said port,
its prospective date of arrival, and the duration
of its stay.

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.96)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
April 10, 1935.
S25
PV136
EHD:
This morning I talked in regard to
this matter with Mr. Holmes of PC. Mr.
Holmes said that the usual and universal
rule was that visits of foreign public
vessels, armed or unarmed, to United States
ports should be notified to the Department
of State through the diplomatic mission of
the nation whose public vessels were con-
cerned; that the only exception was the
arrangement in regard to the Philippine
Islands; and that he thought that it would
be a mistake for the Department to make an
exception to the rule in the case of
Japanese unarmed public vessels visiting
United States ports.
I perceive no need of conferring with
the Navy in regard to this matter and
suggest that Mr. Dooman inform Mr. Kawahara
that after consideration it is felt that
difficulties would ensue in case the usual
and universal procedure were modified,
emphasizing that there is no question of
discrimination involved and that in the case
of public vessels all foreign nations follow
the procedure of giving notice to the Depart-
ment of State through the respective
diplomatic mission.

=== Page 203 === (confidence 0.96)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Apri1 16, 1935.
OUNPRRSATION:
193
AND
DIA
Mr. Shun-ichiro Kawahara,
Division of
CONFERENCES
APR17 1935
APH15 1935
Mr. Dooman.
Department of Stats
Subject:
Entry into American Ports of Japanese Unarmed
Public Vessels.
894.3311
Mr. Kawahara called this morning at my request. I made
known to him the view outlined in Mr. Hamilton's memorandum
894.33
of April 10, 1935, stating in effect that it is our belief
that visits of Japanese public vessels would best be
regulated by conformity by the Japanese to the procedure
applicable to all foreign public vessels -- that notification
of visits be made through diplomatic channels to the
6
Department of State.
7
Mr. Kawahara recalled that I had described as "irregular"
the practice of the Japanese in making notification either
through consular officers to the local authorities or by the
FILED
Japanese Naval Attache to the Navy Department, but he did
not believe that a practice of ten years' standing could
be regarded as "irregular": it was his belief that this
1”
Government had given tacit consent to the Japanese follow-
ing such practice. I observed to Mr. Kawahara that I
could not appreciate the cogency of his statement; I did
not know how long the Japanese had been following such
F
/FG
practice,

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
practice, but assuming that the United States had inter-
posed no objection over a period of one year, five years
or ten years, the right of the United States to prescribe
the precedure which should be followed could not in any
way be regarded as having been in any way limited; and
that we are now requesting that Japanese vessels now conform
their practices to the practices of all other foreign
vessels coming to the United States.
Mr. Kawahara then asked whether the American Government
would be inconvenienced if the Japanese Government were to
give notice of termination of the informal arrangement of
193l (covering visits of Japanese vessels to the Philippine
Islands and of American vessels to Japan). I stated that
that arrangement had been made at the request of the Japa-
nese Government; and that this Government had entered into
the arrangement largely because of desire to accommodate
the wishes of the Japanese Government; that this Government
is satisfied with the terms of the arrangement of 193l; but
that I did not believe we would be greatly inconvenienced
if the arrangement were terminated.
The conversation then turned to other matters.

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
July 15, 1935.
1935 JUL 29 AM 11 44
CONVERSATION:
Mr. Shun-ichiro Kawahara,
ONS
Mr. Sturgeon.
994.3311
T
Subject:
Entry into American Ports of Japanese
Unarmed Public Vessels.
894.33
Mr. Kawahara requested an appointment, and called to
continue a discussion begun with Mr. Dooman in regard to
reciprocal treatment of American and Japanese public
vessels entering the ports of Japan and the United States,
respectively.
207
Mr. Kawahara referred to the existing informal arrange-
ment with this Government whereby Japanese unarmed public
vessels enter the Philippines and American unarmed public
vessels enter ports of Japan upon notification of local
AUC 17 1035
FILED
authorities only. He also mentioned that, although not
formally permitted to do so, Japanese public vessels have
been entering American ports upon notification to local
authorities.
Mr. Kawahara then said that in view of recent conver-
sations looking toward regulation of the entrance of publio
vessels in Japan and the United States, the Embassy had
F/FG
expected to transmit notification of discontinuance of the
present informal agreement between Japan and the United
States pertaining to the Philippines and Japan. However,
Mr. Kawahara

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Mr. Kawahara said, this is not the present idea of the
Japanese Government. It is desired rather that an agree-
ment be reached whereby public vessels registered in the
Navy Department shall enter American ports only after
notice is given through diplomatic channels; while all
other public vessels shall be allowed to enter upon
notification to local authorities.
Mr. Kawahara was asked what categories "all other
public vessels" would include, but lacked information
upon which to make reply. He said that this information
might later be furnished. He was advised that this might
be desirable; and that meantime his statement would be given
appropriate consideration.
LDS/DLY

MEMORANDUM
Yesterday afternoon Commander Hill of
the Navy Department stated that the Navy
Department desired to notify as soon as
possible its officers in the Hawaiian
Islands of the approaching visit to Hilo
of the TAISEI MARU, and he asked whether I
could obtain from the Japanese Embassy some
information in regard to the itinerary of
that vessel.

telephone when the TAISEI MARU was expected
to arrive at Hilo and whether it was expect-
ed that the vessel would enter other American
ports. Mr. Kawahara stated that the vessel
would arrive at Hilo on August 23, that it
would remain there until August 30, on which
date it,would sale direct to Japan.

=== Page 257 === (confidence 0.97)
The Secretary of State presents his compliments to
Their Excellencies and Messieurs the Chiefs of Mission,
and has the honor to bring to their attention the fact
that on several occasions foreign naval vessels and
other public vessels belonging to foreign governments
have entered ports of the United States without prior
notification having been given through customary and
appropriate channels.
In order that proper courtesies and facilities may
be extended by the authorities of this Government to ves-
sels of this character, the Secretary of State has the
honor to request that, except in cases covered by special
arrangements, steps be taken in order that prior notiri-
cation shall be given to the Departmont of State through
diplomatie channels when suah vessels are expected to oall
at porte of the United States or its island possessioms.
Dopartment of state,
ehingtem, Jy 1, 195s.

894.3311/217
The Secretary of State.
Dear Mr. Secretary:
The Acting Secretary of War has requested me to reply
to letter from the Under Secretary of State dated October 30,
1935 (FE 894.3311/215) in which he requested an expression of
the views of the War Department regarding the termination of
informal agreements governing the visits of American unarmed
public vessels to ports in Japan and of Japanese unarmed public
vessels to ports in the Philippine Islands.
I am pleased to advise you that the War Department has
no objection to the termination of the present informal arrange-
ments with the Japanese Government for the visits of American
unarmed public vessels to ports in Japan and for visits of
similar Japanese vessels to ports in the Philippine Islands.
Sincerely yours,
Chief of Staff.

(b) Courtesies and facilities provided: None except

4.
On the occasion of the boarding visit request was
made to Captain Tashiro for a list of the Officers on board. The
Captain stated that he did not have one available, but that one would
be furnished by the Japanese Consulate-General. An officer later
visited the Consulate and was informed that no list of officers on
the TsURUI had been obtained by the Consulate and that they did not
have nowledge of the names of the officers on board. The request
was made clearly and explicitly.
5.
In view of the difficulties that had arisen in the
past regarding rosters or lists of offiers on board Japanese public
vessels entering Hawaiian harbors, the Commandant on 13 September
addressed a letter to the Japanese Consul-General, which contained in
part: "In the case of future visits to the Hawaiian Area by public
vessels or aircraft of the Imperial Japanese Government, it is re-
quested that notification in advance of their arrival be made as has
been the custom in the past in connection with visits of your Naval
vessels, and that a list of officials under your Government, who may
be on board be furnished to the end that appropriate courtesies may
be extended."
6.

Commandant by letter that certain Naval Tankers would call at Honolulu
or at Hilo on their return voyage from Los Angeles to Japan. This

Subject:
H.I.J..D. TURUMI; Visit of.
11.
Additional information acquired on an enlisted
man contaoted while on liberty was as follows:
Ship came in ahead of time due to a good tail
wind. Ship could make faster tine between coast
and Honolulu, but more days are taken because
training and drills are held during the voyage.
People in Japan sympathize with Ethiopia. Within
a few years, Japan will get her oil from Manchuria
and will not have to depend on other nations. Man
did not lnow how much oil the TSURUNI was carrying,
but stated that he thought between four and five
thousand tons of heavy oil.
12.
Captain Tashiro spoke quite halting nglish. The
Secretary of the Jananese Consulate-General stated that Captain
Tashiro had been in Honolulu on one of the training ships that
visited here in June, 1935, hence he nust be new aboard the TS RU.I.
Articles in the Japanese press indicated that he had been Executive
Officer of the YAkUo during her visit to Honolulu.
13.
The following ncident in connection with the visit
of the TSURUI is of interest:
Several Japanese sailors wandered into a kinder-
garten school in onolulu, uninvited and, apparently unaware that
the school was not under Japanese supervision. They spent about
two hours in the school room. They inspected ana seomed very in-
terested in the books being used. They t alled to the childron and
apparently asked many questions, which the chiluren readily answered.
One sailor drew a picture of a Japancse battleship and explained
it to the children. Another drew a picture of an airplane carrier
sinking and laballed it SARAroGA in nglish. nother drew a pic-
ture of a Japanese soldier outting off the head of a man and dem-
onstrated how it was done. The teacher in charge protested at
this and requeated the men to leave, which they did without pro-
test. When questioned by the teacher as to what the men had said,
the children merely giggled and gave no answer.

FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
AJAN 2 2 1936
January 6, 1936.
Department of State
JO NOTS
Subject: Activity of Japanese Oil Tanker.
To:
Assistant Chief of Staff, G-2, War Department,
Washington, D. C.
1. For your information we quote herewith report made
January 3, 1936, by C.I.P. of this office:
894.3311/25
"According to a confidential report made by
Captain Piltz of the cable ship "Dickenson" the
Japanese oil tanker "Tsurumi" on her return trip
894.3311
to Japan during the month of December, 1935, made
nightly observations of activities at Midway and
Wake Island.
"The Japanese oil tanker "Tsurumi" according
to schedule on file in this office, arrived in
5
Honolulu December 5, 1935 and departed for Japan
0
December 7, 1935.
"Captain Piltz reports that the "Tsurumi"
left the port of Honolulu on the same day as the
"Dickenson" and that the "Tsurumi" arrived at
Midway Island at about 1l:00 p.m., two days after
the "Dickenson." Captain Piltz said that his
attention was called to the "Tsurumi" by the
9E61 8
ship's look-out at about ll:00 p.m., while the
Gh Lud
"Dickenson" lay at anchor off Midway Island. The
"Tsurumi" was cruising at about six knots per hour
close to the Island of Midway. The Captain made
several observations of the "Tsurumi" until the
ship was lost to view. He then laid his obser-
vations out on his chart and the plotted course
showed the "Tsurumi" on a direct course for Wake
Island."
2. It is recommended that the matter be turned over to the
State Department, in order that the Japanese Government may be informed
that we are aware of these activities.
101
G. S. PATTON, JR.,
F
Lt. Colonel, General Staff Corps,
G
A. C. of S., G-2.

March 17, 1936
MEMORANDUM OF CONVERSATION WITH THE JAPANESE AMBASSADOR
I sent for the Japanese Ambassador this afternoon
and handed him our memorandum of yesterday's date with
regard to calls of Japanese public vessels at American
ports on the dates scheduled. I said that this was a
very small matter in itself, but the fact that the
Japanese vessels were calling on dates earlier than those
arranged for had been somewhat upsetting to the local
authorities; I expressed the hope, therefore, tnat the
394.3311/
vessels woula not arrive before the prescribed dates.
The Ambassador said that he would at once telegraph
hi: Government ana he seemed to be certain that the
matter would be readily straightened out.
2
266
William Phillips.
U:WP:GMH

and to Ottawa, Canada, fro New York, Nev York, about
